Statistical Inference for Conditional Group Distributionally Robust Optimization with Cross-Entropy Loss
Zijian Guo, Zhenyu Wang, Yifan Hu, Francis Bach

Abstract
In multi-source learning with discrete labels, distributional heterogeneity across domains poses a central challenge to developing predictive models that transfer reliably to unseen domains. We study multi-source unsupervised domain adaptation, where labeled data are drawn from multiple source domains and only unlabeled data from a target domain. To address potential distribution shifts, we propose a novel C onditional G roup D istributionally R obust O ptimization (CG-DRO) framework that learns a classifier by minimizing the worst-case cross-entropy loss over the convex combinations of the conditional outcome distributions from the sources. To solve the resulting minimax problem, we develop an efficient Mirror Prox algorithm, where we employ a double machine learning procedure to estimate the risk function. This ensures that the errors of the machine learning estimators for the nuisance models enter only at higher-order rates, thereby preserving statistical efficiency under covariate shift. We establish fast statistical convergence rates for the estimator by constructing two surrogate minimax optimization problems that serve as theoretical bridges. A distinguishing challenge for CG-DRO is the emergence of nonstandard asymptotics: the empirical estimator may fail to converge to a standard limiting distribution due to boundary effects and system instability. To address this, we introduce a perturbation-based inference procedure that enables uniformly valid inference, including confidence interval construction and hypothesis testing.



1
Introduction

In modern applications, data are frequently collected from multiple sources, such as hospitals, geographic locations, time periods, or experimental batches. These multi-source datasets often exhibit a certain degree of distributional heterogeneity, with each source characterized by potentially different but related distributions. This heterogeneity poses challenges for prediction models trained under the empirical risk minimization (ERM) framework, which tends to perform well on average but may fail to generalize when there are distributional shifts between the source and target domains
(
Koh et al. 2021
;
Malinin et al. 2021
)
. The central objective of multi-source data analysis is to learn a generalizable prediction model that performs reliably not only within the observed domains but also under potential distributional shifts in future or previously unseen domains.

We focus on the multi-source unsupervised domain adaptation regime for multi-category classification: multiple source domains have labeled data, while the target domain only has unlabeled data
(
Duan et al. 2012
;
Sun et al. 2015
;
Perone et al. 2019
)
.
We illustrate this regime in Figure
1
, and the term “unsupervised” refers to the absence of target labels. Such a setting is prevalent in practice, as acquiring outcome labels in the target domain is often costly or time-consuming. Our primary goal is to learn information shared across multiple source domains and transfer such generalizable knowledge to an unlabeled target domain. To achieve this goal, we propose a
C
onditional
G
roup
D
istributionally
R
obust
O
ptimization (CG-DRO) model that optimizes the worst-case risk over the collection of all possible mixture conditional outcome distributions, given feature data from the target domain.

Figure 1
:
Illustration of Multi-source Unsupervised Domain Adaptation. The source domains have labeled data, while the target domain only has unlabeled data.

Classification tasks play a central role in modern applications, with loss functions such as cross-entropy. Despite their widespread use, the theoretical understanding of CG-DRO under cross-entropy loss remains limited. In particular, fundamental questions concerning statistical convergence rates and uncertainty quantification remain largely unaddressed. Existing theoretical results have predominantly focused on regression problems involving the squared loss
(
Meinshausen and Bühlmann 2015
;
Guo 2023
)
, which fail to capture the distinctive challenges of classification under distributional shifts. This paper addresses these gaps by developing a unified theoretical framework for CG-DRO under cross-entropy loss, encompassing logistic loss as a special case. We solve the problem via optimization algorithms, derive sharp statistical convergence rates for the resulting estimators, and propose a method for constructing uniformly valid confidence intervals for the population-level CG-DRO model.

1.1
Our Results and Contributions

We study the multi-source unsupervised domain adaptation setting illustrated in Figure
1
and propose a novel CG-DRO framework to extract a generalizable predictive structure shared across sources. Since we do not have access to samples of the conditional outcome distribution from the target domain, we consider an uncertainty set as mixtures of conditional outcome distributions from the source domains. Thus, our target model is to minimize the expectation over the target features and the worst-case cross-entropy risk over the uncertainty class of the conditional outcome distributions. Unlike the classical Group DRO
(
Hashimoto et al. 2018
;
Sagawa et al. 2019
;
Lahoti et al. 2020
)
, which considers uncertainty classes over joint distributions, our formulation focuses on conditional distributions, motivating the term
Conditional
Group DRO. Our model admits a minimax formulation and encourages identifying stable features that are consistently predictive across all source domains, thereby reducing sensitivity to domain-specific spurious correlations.

The CG-DRO involves a minimax optimization problem, where the maximum is taken to calculate the worst-case loss over the uncertainty set, and the minimization is to search for a model minimizing the worst-case loss.
To solve the empirical version of the CG-DRO, we adopt the Mirror Prox algorithm
(
Juditsky et al. 2011
;
Bubeck 2015
, e.g.)
, which leverages optimistic gradient updates to efficiently handle the minimax optimization problem. To address the covariate shift between the source and target domains, we further develop a double machine learning (or double robustness) estimator
(
Chernozhukov et al. 2018
;
Vansteelandt and Dukes 2022
;
Wang and Shah 2024
, e.g.)
for a key component of the empirical risk. This estimator is integrated into the Mirror Prox algorithm to ensure that the error arising from the machine learning estimation of nuisance models enters only at a higher-order rate.

While it is well established that the Mirror Prox algorithm converges to the optimal solution of the empirical CG-DRO problem, there is a significant lack of theoretical understanding for this optimal solution’s statistical behavior,
particularly its statistical convergence rate and limiting distribution. We close this gap by developing a unified theoretical framework that characterizes the statistical properties of the empirical CG-DRO estimator, defined as the solution to the empirical problem. In particular, we derive sharp finite-sample convergence rates for this estimator relative to its population counterpart. Standard M-estimation theory does not directly yield fast parametric rates in this setting, as the maximization taken after the expectation introduces non-smoothness into the primal objective function. To overcome this challenge, we construct two carefully designed surrogate minimax problems that approximate the empirical and population CG-DRO problems. These surrogates serve as analytical intermediaries, enabling us to derive fast statistical convergence rates for the empirical estimator. As a key technical contribution, we characterize the discrepancy between the solutions of the surrogate and original minimax problems in Theorem
1
. These theoretical tools may be of independent interest beyond the current setting.

Statistical inference for the CG-DRO model is an important yet challenging task. Our proposed CG-DRO model is interpreted as a generalizable model capturing stable predictive patterns across diverse environments, making it a robust and meaningful choice for deployment in new target domains. This makes testing whether a certain element of the CG-DRO model is significant an important task, i.e., whether the corresponding coordinate is zero. However, as illustrated in Figure
4
, the empirical CG-DRO estimator typically does not admit an asymptotically normal limiting distribution, complicating standard inferential approaches. This nonstandard behavior arises in at least two common scenarios: (i) when the source domains exhibit highly similar conditional outcome distributions, leading to instability in solving the empirical problem; and (ii) when the shifts between conditional outcome distributions are sparse but pronounced, resulting in one domain receiving a dominating weight and inducing boundary effects that complicate inference. To address these challenges, we propose a novel perturbation-based inferential procedure for quantifying uncertainty in the CG-DRO estimator. We show that the resulting confidence intervals are uniformly valid and achieve parametric length, even in settings where the CG-DRO estimator fails to exhibit an asymptotically normal distribution. This provides a principled and flexible framework for inference in minimax optimization problems.

To summarize, the main contributions of this paper are threefold:

1.

We propose a CG-DRO framework that extracts stable predictive structure across source domains under cross-entropy loss, and solve the resulting minimax problem via a Mirror Prox algorithm enhanced with a
D
ouble
M
achine
L
earning (DML) estimator.

2.

We establish a fast statistical convergence rate for the CG-DRO estimator by constructing surrogate minimax problems that serve as analytical bridges.

3.

We develop a perturbation-based inference method that yields uniformly valid confidence intervals with parametric length for the CG-DRO model, even when its empirical estimator does not admit a standard normal limiting distribution.

1.2
Additional Related Literature

Maximin Effect and CG-DRO for Least-Square Loss.

This paper is closely related to research on the maximin effect in the no-covariate-shift setting
(
Meinshausen and Bühlmann 2015
;
Bühlmann and Meinshausen 2015
;
Rothenhäusler et al. 2016
)
, the CG-DRO framework under covariate shift
(
Guo 2023
;
Wang et al. 2023
)
, and minimax optimization with regret loss
(
Mo et al. 2024
;
Zhang et al. 2024b
)
. These works define minimax optimization by maximizing worst-case squared losses, and a key aspect of their analysis is that optimal solutions have closed-form expressions.
In contrast, our paper focuses on classification tasks with cross-entropy loss, where closed-form solutions are unavailable. This means existing analytical and inferential methods do not directly apply. To overcome these challenges, we construct surrogate minimax optimization problems to facilitate theoretical analysis and build perturbation-based confidence intervals.

Group DRO.

Group Distributionally Robust Optimization approaches typically build generalizable prediction models using source domain data without target domain information
(
Sagawa et al. 2019
;
Lahoti et al. 2020
)
. More recently, in the context of online data,
Zhang et al. 2024c
proposed a novel algorithm that achieves optimal sample complexity for solving Group DRO. Our work, however, focuses on unsupervised domain adaptation, leveraging available target covariates, as illustrated in Figure
1
. This extra information allows us to design a distributionally robust model specifically tailored for the target domain. A more detailed comparison is in Section
2.1
.

Similarity-based Multi-source Learning.
Apart from Group DRO, a broader body of work in multi-source learning aims to identify generalizable patterns from heterogeneous data sources, by assuming certain types of shared structure across domains. Notable examples include transfer learning
(
Li et al. 2022
;
Tian and Feng 2023
;
Xiong et al. 2023
)
, meta-analysis
(
Sun and Xia 2024
;
Guo et al. 2025
)
and multi-task learning
(
Duan and Wang 2023
)
.
In contrast, our proposed CG-DRO framework does not require such similarity conditions and offers a distinct approach to learning generalizable models.

Nonregular Inference.
Nonregular (or nonstandard) inference frequently arises in modern data science, where estimators do not admit standard limiting distributions (e.g., asymptotically normal), and hence classical inference tools based on such approximations fail to apply
(
Wasserman et al. 2020
;
Xie and Wang 2024
;
Kuchibhotla et al. 2021
;
Guo 2023
;
Guo et al. 2025
)
. We demonstrate in Section
5.1
that the CG-DRO estimator exhibits non-regular behavior due to boundary effects and/or system singularity. While classical subsampling and bootstrap methods fail in such non-regular settings due to boundary effects
(
Andrews 1999
)
, we propose a novel perturbation-based inference procedure that guarantees valid coverage even when the estimator deviates from asymptotic normality.

Double Machine Learning (DML).
DML provides a powerful framework for incorporating nonparametric estimation of nuisance components while still allowing valid inference for parameters of interest; see
Chernozhukov et al. 2018
;
Wang and Shah 2024
;
Vansteelandt and Dukes 2022
and references therein. In this work, we extend the DML framework beyond its traditional focus on low-dimensional target parameters. Specifically, we use DML to construct an unbiased estimator of a risk function in settings with covariate shift. This shift in perspective, from estimating a finite-dimensional parameter to debiasing a full risk function, enables us to analyze a novel empirical minimax optimization problem based on this debiased risk. Our approach highlights how DML ideas can be leveraged not only for inference but also as a tool for robust optimization under distributional shift.

Algorithms for Minimax Optimization.

Minimax optimization employs established algorithms like gradient descent maximization (updating the minimizer after finding a near-optimal maximizer) and gradient descent ascent (updating both variables simultaneously or alternately)
(
Lin et al. 2020
)
. These have been extended to stochastic settings
(
Lin et al. 2020
;
Yang et al. 2020
)
. For a summary of optimization convergence results, see
Zhang and Hu 2025
. We utilize the Mirror Prox algorithm
(
Bubeck 2015
;
Mokhtari et al. 2020b
;
Mokhtari et al. 2020a
)
to solve the CG-DRO model. Our primary algorithmic contribution is integrating a DML estimator into the risk function. Our analysis focuses on the statistical convergence rate and uncertainty quantification of the minimax solution, rather than optimization convergence guarantees.

Generalization Bounds for Stochastic Minimax Optimization.

Research on stochastic minimax optimization also addresses generalization performance and statistical rates. For example,
Zhang et al. 2021
examined the stability of empirical minimax optimization and established generalization bounds.
Zhang et al. 2022
extended this by combining these results with optimization convergence rates to derive bounds for complex algorithms. More recently,
Boob and Guzmán 2024
established stability and generalization bounds for the extra-gradient method. However, these existing results are not applicable to our setting due to their reliance on a strong concavity assumption for the inner maximization problem. When this assumption is violated, as in our case, the maximization problem may have multiple optimal solutions, deteriorating the convergence rate
(
Zhang et al. 2024a
)
. To overcome this, we develop surrogate optimization problems as theoretical bridges for our analysis. Additionally, our approach incorporates a DML estimator for the empirical objective, unlike prior work that assumes direct access to a simple sample-average empirical objective.

Statistical Inference via Optimization Methods.

Our work relates to statistical inference for optimization methods. Early work by
Chen et al. 2020
established inference for stochastic gradient methods under strong convexity and smoothness, later generalized by
Davis et al. 2024
for non-smooth objectives. Further works investigated statistical inference for covariance estimation using gradient methods
(
Jiang et al. 2025
)
and constrained stochastic optimization
(
Duchi and Ruan 2021
;
Du et al. 2025
)
. However, these analyses are not directly applicable to our minimax problem, and Figure
4
empirically shows that the limiting distribution of the empirical CG-DRO model is not normal. In contrast, we develop a novel inferential strategy for minimax optimization. We first quantify uncertainty from the inner maximization using a novel perturbation approach, then perform inference on the outer minimization via M-estimation. This decoupling overcomes the challenge of directly analyzing the full minimax solution, thereby enabling the construction of valid confidence intervals in the presence of a nonstandard limiting distribution.

1.3
Notations

For a positive integer
m
, we define
[m]=\{1,2,...,m\}
. For a real value
x
, we denote by
x_{m}
the vector with all entries equal to
x
, and let
\mathbf{I}_{m}
be the

m\times m
identity matrix. For a matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, we denote its singular values by
\sigma_{1}(A)\geq...\geq\sigma_{p}(A)\geq 0
with
p=\min\{m,n\}
, its spectral norm by
\|A\|_{2}
, and its Frobenius norm by
\|A\|_{F}
. For a symmetric matrix
A
, we use
\lambda_{\min}(A)
and
\lambda_{\max}(A)
to denote its smallest and largest eigenvalues, respectively. We write
A\preceq B
(or
B\succeq A
) if
B-A
is positive semidefinite, for two symetric matrices
A,B
.
For a vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, its
\ell_{p}
-norm (
p\geq 1
) is given by
\|x\|_{p}=(\sum_{j=1}^{d}|x_{j}|^{p})^{1/p}
.
For a sequence
x_{n}
, we write
x_{n}\xlongrightarrow{p}x
to denote convergence in probability, and
x_{n}\xlongrightarrow{d}x
for convergence in distribution. For positive sequences
a(n)
and
b(n)
, we write
a(n)\lesssim b(n)
or
a(n)=\mathcal{O}(b(n))
if there exists a constant
C>0
such that
a(n)\leq C\cdot b(n)
for all

n\geq 1
, and write
a(n)\asymp b(n)
if both
a(n)\lesssim b(n)
and
b(n)\lesssim a(n)
. We use
a(n)\ll b(n)
,
a(n)=o(b(n))
or
b(n)\gg a(n)
when
\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}(a(n)/b(n))=0
.
Throughout, we use
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},C
to denote generic positive constants that may vary from place to place.

2
Conditional Group Distributionally Robust Optimization

In this section, we introduce the multi-source unsupervised domain adaptation regime and propose the CG-DRO model to learn the generalizable information shared across domains. For the
l
-th source dataset with
1\leq l\leq L
, we have access to independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) observations
\{X_{i}^{(l)},Y_{i}^{(l)}\}_{1\leq i\leq n_{l}}
sampled from the joint distribution
\mathbb{P}^{(l)}=(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{(l)},\mathbb{P}_{Y|X}^{(l)})
, where
X_{i}^{(l)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
Y^{(l)}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
denote the covariates and the outcome, respectively. In the target domain, covariates
\{X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
are i.i.d. generated from
\mathbb{Q}_{X}
, while the corresponding outcome variables remain unobserved.

We capture distributional differences across sources by allowing both covariate shift and conditional outcome shift between the source and target domains.
Specifically, the target covariate distribution
\mathbb{Q}_{X}
may differ from any of
\{\mathbb{P}_{X}^{(l)}\}_{1\leq l\leq L}
, and similarly, the conditional distribution
\mathbb{Q}_{Y|X}
may differ from any of
\{\mathbb{P}_{Y|X}^{(l)}\}_{1\leq l\leq L}
.
We refer to the special case where
\mathbb{Q}_{X}=\mathbb{P}_{X}^{(l)}
for all
1\leq l\leq L
as the no covariate shift setting.

The primary objective of multi-source unsupervised domain adaptation is to extract transferable knowledge shared across source domains and adapt it to the target domain. To achieve this, we propose a CG-DRO model that guarantees robust performance over a family of plausible target distributions. While
\mathbb{Q}_{X}
is identifiable through observed covariates, the conditional distribution
\mathbb{Q}_{Y|X}
remains non-identifiable without additional structural assumptions. Instead of making identifiability assumptions, we construct an uncertainty class encapsulating potential target distributions:

\mathcal{C}=\left\{(\mathbb{Q}_{X},\mathbb{T}_{Y|X}):\mathbb{T}_{Y|X}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\gamma_{l}\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{Y|X},\;\text{with}\;\gamma\in\Delta^{L}\right\},

(1)

where
\Delta^{L}
denotes the
(L-1)
-dimensional simplex. This class
\mathcal{C}
contains the true target distribution
(\mathbb{Q}_{X},\mathbb{Q}_{Y|X})
if
\mathbb{Q}_{Y|X}
admits a mixture representation of the source conditional distributions. Given the distributional uncertainty, we define the worst-case risk:

\max_{\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{C}}\ \mathbb{E}_{(X,Y)\sim\mathbb{T}}\ \ell(X,Y,\theta),

(2)

where
\mathbb{E}_{(X,Y)\sim\mathbb{T}}\ \ell(X,Y,\theta)
denotes a general risk function with the covariates
X
and outcome
Y
generating from the distribution
\mathbb{T}
and
\theta
parameterizing the prediction model. We define the CG-DRO model
\theta^{*}
, as the solution to the following minimax optimization,

\theta^{*}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta}\ \max_{\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{C}}\ \mathbb{E}_{(X,Y)\sim\mathbb{T}}\ \ell(X,Y,\theta).

(3)

When
\ell(X,Y,\theta)
is strictly convex in
\theta
, the maximum risk
\max_{\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{C}}\ \mathbb{E}_{(X,Y)\sim\mathbb{T}}\ \ell(X,Y,\theta)
inherits its strict convexity, ensuring a unique solution of
\theta^{*}
in (
4
).
Importantly, we interpret
\theta^{*}
as a transferable prediction model since it checks through all possible target distributions belonging to
\mathcal{C}
and optimizes the worst-case risk function.
Leveraging the mixture distribution definition used in (
1
), we reformulate (
3
) as:

\theta^{*}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta}\ \max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\gamma_{l}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathbb{Q}_{X}}\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{Y|X}}\ell(X,Y,\theta).

(4)

2.1
Comparison to Group DRO and ERM

We now review the related Group DRO works
(
Sagawa et al. 2019
;
Lahoti et al. 2020
)
and further compare our CG-DRO model to Group DRO and ERM in a numerical study. Our proposed model is different from Group DRO in the sense that Group DRO is designed for settings only with data from multiple sources, but not any target data.
Group DRO defines the uncertainty class comprising all mixtures of the joint distributions
(X,Y)
from the sources,

\mathcal{C}_{\rm GDRO}=\left\{\mathbb{T}:\mathbb{T}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\gamma_{l}\cdot\mathbb{P}^{(l)},\;\gamma\in\Delta^{L}\right\}.

With the above uncertainty class,
Group DRO solves the following robust prediction model:

\displaystyle\theta^{*}_{\rm GDRO}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\ \max_{\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{C}_{\rm GDRO}}\ \mathbb{E}_{(X,Y)\sim\mathbb{T}}\ell(X,Y,\theta).

(5)

The uncertainty class
\mathcal{C}_{\rm GDRO}
considers all possible mixtures of the full joint distributions across sources, whereas our uncertainty class
\mathcal{C}
in (
1
) focuses on mixtures of the conditional outcome distributions, holding the target covariate distribution
\mathbb{Q}_{X}
fixed.
When the covariate distributions align, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}_{X}^{(l)}=\mathbb{Q}_{X}
for all
1\leq l\leq L
, the two uncertainty classes coincide. In this case, the distributionally robust prediction model in (
5
) reduces to our formulation in (
3
).
However, under general covariate shift, the two models typically yield different solutions.

We utilize a numerical example to illustrate the benefits of our CG-DRO framework compared to both the Group DRO method in (
5
) and the ERM approach that fits a model on the pooled source data. The setting involves
L=2
source domains and one target domain under covariate shift, i.e.
\mathbb{Q}_{X}\neq\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{X}
for
l=1,2
; see Appendix
E.1
for the detailed setup. We vary the source mixture by adjusting the proportion of samples from the first source across
\{0.1,0.2,\dots,0.9\}
. All methods are evaluated based on their worst-case loss as defined in (
2
).

As shown in Figure
2
, the proposed CG-DRO method consistently achieves lower worst-case loss than both Group DRO and ERM, demonstrating its robustness to distributional shifts between the source and target domains. While the model learned by ERM is sensitive to the source mixture, both Group DRO and our method remain stable across different proportions. Notably,
CG-DRO outperforms Group DRO, as it is specifically designed to ensure robustness with respect to the target domain by leveraging the unlabeled target data during training. As the benchmarks, Figure
2
also includes the non-reducible losses for the two source domains, each computed under its own distribution using the corresponding true conditional distributions
\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{Y|X}
for
l=1,2
; see the exact formula in Appendix
E.1
.

Figure 2
:
Comparison of the worst-case loss defined in (
2
) between CG-DRO, Group DRO, and ERM across varying source mixture weights. The non-reducible losses are computed using the true conditional distributions
\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{Y|X}
, for
l=1,2
.

2.2
CG-DRO with cross-entropy loss

In this subsection, we focus on the commonly used cross-entropy loss function for categorical outcome variables. We consider the categorical outcome variable
Y
taking values from
\{0,1,\dots,K\}
with
K\geq 1
, with the binary classification setting corresponding to the special case with
K=1
.
We define the cross-entropy loss function as,

\ell(X,Y,\theta)=-\sum_{c=0}^{K}{\bf 1}(Y=c)\log\left[\frac{\exp(\theta_{c}^{\intercal}X)}{1+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\exp(\theta_{k}^{\intercal}X)}\right],

where each class-specific parameter vector
\theta_{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for
c\in\{0,1,...,K\}
. For identifiability, we set class
c=0
as the reference category and fix
\theta_{0}
as a zero vector. We then stack the remaining parameters into a single vector
\theta=(\theta_{1}^{\intercal},\theta_{2}^{\intercal},\dots,\theta_{K}^{\intercal})^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}
, which represents the parameters of the predictive model.
In the context of cross-entropy loss, we further simplify the CG-DRO model in (
4
) as follows,

\theta^{*}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\ \max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\ \phi(\theta,\gamma)\quad\text{with}\quad\phi(\theta,\gamma)\coloneqq\sum_{l=1}^{L}\gamma_{l}\cdot\theta^{\intercal}\mu^{(l)}+S(\theta),

(6)

where
\mu^{(l)}=\left([\mu^{(l)}_{1}]^{\intercal},[\mu^{(l)}_{2}]^{\intercal},\cdots,[\mu^{(l)}_{K}]^{\intercal}\right)^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}
with
\{\mu^{(l)}_{c}\}_{1\leq c\leq K}
defined as,

\mu^{(l)}_{c}=-\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathbb{Q}_{X}}\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{Y|X}}\left[{\bf 1}(Y=c)\cdot X\right]\quad\text{and}\quad S(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathbb{Q}_{X}}\log\left[{1+\sum_{c=1}^{K}\exp(\theta_{c}^{\intercal}X)}\right].

(7)

We establish in Lemma
1
of Appendix that
\phi(\theta,\gamma)
is strictly convex towards
\theta
for any
\gamma
, implying that
\theta^{*}
in (
6
) is uniquely defined.
In the above definition,
\mu^{(l)}_{c}
measures the association between the one-hot encoding
{\bf 1}(Y=c)
and
X\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The vector
\mu^{(l)}
is constructed by stacking the class-specific vectors
\{\mu^{(l)}_{c}\}_{1\leq c\leq K}
and does not depend on
\theta
. We separate the log sum exponential function
S(\theta)
as it does not depend on the source index
l
.

It is essential to note that our analysis does not rely on specifying a conditional mean model for each source distribution. Instead, we define
\theta^{*}
as the optimal population-level solution to the distributionally robust optimization problem formulated in (
6
).

Remark 1

(CG-DRO with Prior)
.

The CG-DRO model
\theta^{*}
defined in (
4
) might become conservative for a particular target domain since the uncertainty class
\mathcal{C}
may contain many plausible target distributions. We may improve the model’s predictiveness by leveraging available prior information. In particular, we refine the uncertainty in (
1
) by incorporating the prior information
\mathcal{H}
of the mixture, i.e.,

\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}=\left\{(\mathbb{Q}_{X},\mathbb{T}_{Y|X}):\mathbb{T}_{Y|X}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\gamma_{l}\cdot\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{Y|X},\;\gamma\in\mathcal{H}\right\}.

With this refined uncertainty class
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
, we define
\theta^{*}_{\mathcal{H}}
as the solution to (
4
) with replacing
\mathcal{C}
with
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
and obtain the following simplification using the cross-entropy loss,

\theta^{*}_{\mathcal{H}}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ \max_{\gamma\in\mathcal{H}}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\gamma_{l}\cdot\theta^{\intercal}\mu^{(l)}+S(\theta).

3
Mirror Prox Algorithm with Double Machine Learning

In this section, we formulate and solve the empirical CG-DRO model utilizing the observed data.
We first substitute the population-level quantities
S(\theta)
and
\{\mu^{(l)}\}_{1\leq l\leq L}
in (
6
) with their data-dependent estimators
\widehat{S}(\theta)
and
\{\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}\}_{1\leq l\leq L}
, respectively. We then construct the empirical CG-DRO model
\widehat{\theta}
as the solution to the following optimization problem:

\widehat{\theta}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\ \max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\ \widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma)\quad\text{with}\quad\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma):=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\gamma_{l}\cdot\theta^{\intercal}\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}+\widehat{S}(\theta),

(8)

where
\widehat{S}(\theta)
is computed as the sample average over the unlabeled target data
\{X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
.
We introduce the estimators
\{\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}\}_{1\leq l\leq L}
in Sections
3.1
and
3.2
, where their form differs depending on whether covariate shift is present.

This section is organized as follows. In Section
3.1
, we derive explicit expressions for
\{\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}\}_{1\leq l\leq L}
for the setting without covariate shift in (
12
), and introduce the optimistic-gradient Mirror Prox algorithm to solve the empirical problem (
8
). Section
3.2
involves the general covariate shift regime, for which we construct DML estimators
\{\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}\}_{1\leq l\leq L}
in (
14
) and develop a corresponding Mirror Prox algorithm with key components estimated by DML.

3.1
Mirror Prox in No Covariate Shift Regime

In the no covariate shift setting, with
\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{X}=\mathbb{Q}_{X}
for each source
1\leq l\leq L
, we have access to
\{X_{i}^{(l)},Y_{i}^{(l)}\}_{1\leq i\leq n_{l}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{X},\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{Y|X}\right)
, whose distribution is the same as
\left(\mathbb{Q}_{X},\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{Y|X}\right)
. By the definitions of
\mu^{(l)}_{c}
and
S(\theta)
in (
7
), we construct the following unbiased estimators of
\mu^{(l)}_{c}
and
\widehat{S}(\theta)
,

\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}_{c}=-\frac{1}{n_{l}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{l}}{\bf 1}(Y^{(l)}_{i}=c)\cdot X^{(l)}_{i}\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq c\leq K.

(9)

\widehat{S}(\theta)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log\left({1+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\exp(\theta_{k}^{\intercal}X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{i})}\right).

(10)

Given the expressions of
\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}
in (
9
) and
\widehat{S}(\theta)
in (
10
), we evaluate the objective
\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma)
defined in (
8
), which is strictly convex in
\theta
and linear (and thus concave) in
\gamma
.

We now present the optimistic-gradient Mirror Prox algorithm for solving the empirical problem in (
8
), as detailed in Algorithm
1
. Each iteration consists of two sequential steps: an
intermediate step
that computes an intermediate point to better estimate the gradient direction, followed by a
correction step
that updates from the current iterate using gradients evaluated at the intermediate point. The literature has shown that this two-step procedure effectively reduces oscillations near saddle points and achieves faster convergence rates in smooth convex-concave settings
(
Nemirovski 2004
;
Bubeck 2015
;
Mokhtari et al. 2020a
)
.

Data:

Estimated
\{\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}\}_{l\in[L]}
; target covariates
\{X_{j}^{\mathbb{Q}}\}_{j\in[N]}
; learning rates for Mirror Prox:
\eta_{\theta},\eta_{\gamma}
; maximum iteration
T
, tolerance
\epsilon
.

Result:

Mirror Prox estimators
\widehat{\theta}_{T}
,
\widehat{\gamma}_{T}
.

Set
\theta_{0}=\bar{\theta}_{0}=0
,
\gamma_{0}=\bar{\gamma}_{0}=(1/L,\cdots,1/L)
, and
{\rm gap}=1
;

while

0\leq t\leq T
and
{\rm diff}\geq\epsilon

do

Intermediate Step
:

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}_{t+1}

\displaystyle=\theta_{t}-\eta_{\theta}\cdot\nabla_{\theta}\widehat{\phi}(\bar{\theta}_{t},\bar{\gamma}_{t}),\;\;\textrm{and}\;\;\bar{\gamma}_{t+1,l}=\frac{\gamma_{t,l}\cdot\exp\left(\eta_{\gamma}\cdot\left[\nabla_{\gamma}\widehat{\phi}(\bar{\theta}_{t},\bar{\gamma}_{t})\right]_{l}\right)}{\sum_{j=1}^{L}\gamma_{t,j}\cdot\exp\left(\eta_{\gamma}\cdot\left[\nabla_{\gamma}\widehat{\phi}(\bar{\theta}_{t},\bar{\gamma}_{t})\right]_{j}\right)}.

Correction Step
:

\displaystyle{\theta}_{t+1}

\displaystyle=\theta_{t}-\eta_{\theta}\cdot\nabla_{\theta}\widehat{\phi}(\bar{\theta}_{t+1},\bar{\gamma}_{t+1}),\;\;\textrm{and}\;\;{\gamma}_{t+1,l}=\frac{\gamma_{t,l}\cdot\exp\left(\eta_{\gamma}\cdot\left[\nabla_{\gamma}\widehat{\phi}(\bar{\theta}_{t+1},\bar{\gamma}_{t+1})\right]_{l}\right)}{\sum_{j=1}^{L}\gamma_{t,j}\cdot\exp\left(\eta_{\gamma}\cdot\left[\nabla_{\gamma}\widehat{\phi}(\bar{\theta}_{t+1},\bar{\gamma}_{t+1})\right]_{j}\right)}.

Set
\widehat{\theta}_{t}=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\bar{\theta}_{s}
, and
\widehat{\gamma}_{t}=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\bar{\gamma}_{s}
.

Evaluate the duality gap:
{\rm gap}=\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}(\widehat{\theta}_{t},\gamma)-\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{Kd}}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\widehat{\gamma}_{t})
.
end while

Denote
\widehat{\theta}_{T}=\widehat{\theta}_{t}
and
\widehat{\gamma}_{T}=\widehat{\gamma}_{t}
, after convergence.

Algorithm 1

Data-dependent Mirror Prox Algorithm

Specifically, at iteration
t
, the
intermediate step
computes
(\bar{\theta}_{t+1},\bar{\gamma}_{t+1})
synchronously. We update
\bar{\theta}_{t+1}
via the standard gradient descent, while
\bar{\gamma}_{t+1}
is computed using a mirror ascent step. The mirror update generalizes the standard gradient update by replacing the Euclidean geometry with the non-Euclidean geometry that better respects the constraint structure. When the constraint is the simplex, as in our case, the mirror update admits a closed form, which avoids costly projection operations. In addition, the optimization convergence rate of gradient-based methods typically depends on the Lipschitz constant of the objective function measured in the
\ell_{2}
norm, which grows with the dimension, the mirror update based on
\ell_{\infty}
geometry exploits a dimension-independent Lipschitz constant, thus our method achieves faster optimization convergence compared to the standard gradient updates
(
Bubeck 2015
)
.

In the subsequent
correction step
, the iterate
(\theta_{t+1},\gamma_{t+1})
is computed using gradients evaluated at the intermediate point
(\bar{\theta}_{t+1},\bar{\gamma}_{t+1})
. We emphasize that both steps at iteration
t
are initialized from the same previous iterate
(\theta_{t},\gamma_{t})
.
Note that the gradient computed for the correction step at the current iteration is reusable for the immediate step in the next iteration. It implies that at each iteration except for the first one, one only needs to compute the gradient once. This design contrasts with the extra-gradient Mirror Prox algorithm, which requires two gradient computations per iteration: one at
(\theta_{t},\gamma_{t})
in the intermediate step, and another at
(\bar{\theta}_{t+1},\bar{\gamma}_{t+1})
in the correction step
(
Mokhtari et al. 2020a
;
Mokhtari et al. 2020b
)
.

Upon convergence at iteration
T
, we take the average of all the intermediate iterates
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bar{\theta}_{t}
as the output in Algorithm
1
. We denote
\widehat{\theta}_{T}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bar{\theta}_{t}
. It has been established in the literature
(
Bubeck 2015
;
Mokhtari et al. 2020a
;
Wang et al. 2025
)
that the optimistic-gradient Mirror Prox algorithm achieves the following bound, in smooth convex-concave settings, as in our case,

0\leq\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}(\widehat{\theta}_{T},\gamma)-\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{Kd}}\left[\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma)\right]\leq\frac{C}{T},

(11)

where
C>0
is a constant and
T
denotes the number of iterations. Consequently, Algorithm
1
globally solves the empirical problem (
8
) with an error that decays at the rate
1/T
. That is, when
T
is sufficiently large, Algorithm
1
’s output
\widehat{\theta}_{T}
converges to the empirical CG-DRO model
\widehat{\theta}
in (
8
). We provide more discussions of this rate (
11
) in Appendix
B.1
.

We now detail the selection of learning rates
\eta_{\theta},\eta_{\gamma}
used in Algorithm
1
. We notice that the choice of these learning rates mainly affects the practical convergence speed of the optimization algorithm, i.e.,
\|\widehat{\theta}_{T}-\widehat{\theta}\|_{2}
, while it does not directly affect the statistical convergence rate
\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}
, where
\widehat{\theta}_{T}
denotes Algorithm
1
’s output,
\widehat{\theta}
and
\theta^{*}
are the empirical and population CG-DRO models defined in (
8
) and (
6
), respectively. This fundamentally differs from the selection of the tuning parameters in the statistical analysis, where the statistical convergence rate depends on the choice of the tuning parameters, such as the penalty level in the penalized regression. Moreover, in practice, we can monitor the algorithm’s optimization convergence using the duality gap evaluated during the algorithm; see more discussions in Appendix
B.1
. For practical implementation, we set
\eta_{\theta}=\eta
and
\eta_{\gamma}={\eta}/{\log L}
with
\eta\in[0.05,0.2]
, which produced stable and sufficiently fast convergence throughout our simulated experiments in Section
7
.
Furthermore, one could also further explore the adaptive learning rate scheme proposed in
Bach and Levy 2019
, which in practice can accelerate convergence by adjusting to the local smoothness of the objective.

3.2
Covariate-shift Regime: DML Estimator of
\mu^{(l)}

We now address the general setting with covariate shifts between the target and source domains. Unlike the non-shift regime discussed previously in Section
3.1
, the paired data
\{X_{i}^{(l)},Y_{i}^{(l)}\}_{1\leq i\leq n_{l}}\sim\left(\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{X},\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{Y|X}\right)
are biased samples of the distribution
\left(\mathbb{Q}_{X},\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{Y|X}\right)
due to the possible covariate shift. To address this, we introduce a DML estimator
\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}
of
\mu^{(l)}
using the cross-fitting technique
(
Robins et al. 1994
;
Bang and Robins 2005
;
Chernozhukov et al. 2018
)
. We then implement the Mirror Prox algorithm in Algorithm
1
with this refined estimator
\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}
.

The construction of
\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}
involves estimating two nuisance components, which will be specified in the following discussion. The key advantage of the DML is to ensure that the nuisance estimation errors enter only at a higher-order rate, ensuring that
\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}
remains an accurate estimator of
\mu^{(l)}
, even under covariate shift. Consequently, the empirical CG-DRO model
\widehat{\theta}
also remains close to the population counterpart
\theta^{*}
, which are defined through
\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}
and
\mu^{(l)}
in (
8
) and (
6
), respectively.

We provide the main idea of constructing the DML estimator by using the following equivalent definition of
\mu^{(l)}_{c}
in (
7
): for each category
c=1,...,K
,

\mu^{(l)}_{c}=-\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}\sim\mathbb{Q}_{X}}\left[f^{(l)}_{c}(X_{i})\cdot X_{i}\right]\quad\text{with}\quad f^{(l)}_{c}(X_{i})\coloneqq\mathbb{P}^{(l)}(Y_{i}=c\mid X_{i}).

(12)

A naive approach is to estimate
f^{(l)}_{c}(\cdot)
with a Machine Learning (ML) approach, and then plug in this estimated
\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(\cdot)
to construct an initial estimator. However, such a plug-in estimator suffers from a dominating bias component resulting from the error
\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(\cdot)-f^{(l)}_{c}(\cdot)
.
To mitigate this issue, we estimate and correct the leading bias component. This bias correction step leads to a refined estimator of
{\mu}^{(l)}
by reducing the estimation error
\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(\cdot)-f^{(l)}_{c}(\cdot)
to a higher-order term. We now provide more details about our two-step procedure.

Step 1: Plug-in ML estimator.
We start with estimating

\mu^{(l)}_{c}
by plugging in a ML estimator of
f^{(l)}_{c}
into (
12
). Particularly, for each source domain
l\in[L]
, we use the labeled source data
\{X^{(l)}_{i},Y^{(l)}_{i}\}_{i\in[n_{l}]}
to fit the conditional probability model
f^{(l)}
using a preferred ML classification method (e.g., multinomial logistic regression, random forest classifier, neural networks). The resulting fitted model is denoted by
\widehat{f}^{(l)}(x)=\left(\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{1}(x),...,\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{K}(x)\right)
, where each
\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(x)
is the predicted probability that
x
is classified in the category
c\in\{1,...,K\}
. We further construct the plug-in estimator of
\mu_{c}^{(l)}
as
-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j})\cdot X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j}
, using the unlabeled target data
\{X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j}\}_{j\in[N]}
. We decompose the estimation error
-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j})\cdot X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j}-\mu^{(l)}_{c}(x)
as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left[-\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j})\cdot X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}_{X}}\left[-\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(X)\cdot X\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}_{X}}\left[\left(f^{(l)}_{c}(X)-\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(X)\right)\cdot X\right].

(13)

In the above decomposition, the term
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left[-\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j})\cdot X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}_{X}}\left[-\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(X)\cdot X\right]
measures the discrepancy between the finite-sample average and the corresponding population mean. This term admits the convergence rate of
1/\sqrt{N}
and cannot be further improved. However, the term
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}_{X}}\left[\left(f^{(l)}_{c}(X)-\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(X)\right)\cdot X\right]
arises from the estimation error in the ML prediction model. We refer to this term as the bias component, and we shall estimate the bias component in the second step and further correct the plug-in estimator of
\mu^{(l)}
.

Step 2: Correct the bias of the plug-in estimator.
We now explain how to obtain an accurate estimator of the last term in (
13
), which is subsequently used to correct the plug-in estimator of
\mu^{(l)}_{c}
.
Suppose that
\mathbb{Q}_{X}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{X}
for
l\in[L]
. We define the density ratio
\omega^{(l)}(x)=d\mathbb{Q}_{X}(x)/d\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{X}(x)
. For
(X,Y)
drawn from the distribution
\mathbb{P}^{(l)}=(\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{X},\mathbb{P}_{Y|X}^{(l)})
and independent of the fitted
\widehat{f}^{(l)}
, we obtain the following by taking the conditional expectation of the outcome variable,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(X,Y)\sim(\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{X},\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{Y|X})}\left[\omega^{(l)}(X)\cdot({\bf 1}(Y=c)-\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(X))\cdot X\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{X}}\left[\omega^{(l)}(X)\cdot(f^{(l)}_{c}(X)-\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(X))\cdot X\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}_{X}}\left[(f^{(l)}_{c}(X)-\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(X))\cdot X\right],

where the last step follows from the definition of the density ratio
\omega^{(l)}
.
Since the
l
-th source data
\{X^{(l)}_{i},Y^{(l)}_{i}\}_{i\in[n_{l}]}
are i.i.d. drawn from the distribution
\mathbb{P}^{(l)}
, the above equation indicates the following estimator of bias,

\frac{1}{n_{l}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{l}}\left[\widehat{\omega}^{(l)}(X^{(l)}_{i})\cdot({\bf 1}(Y^{(l)}_{i}=c)-\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(X^{(l)}_{i}))\cdot X^{(l)}_{i}\right]\approx\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}_{X}}\left[(f^{(l)}_{c}(X)-\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(X))\cdot X\right].

We now apply the above intuition to design our proposed bias-corrected estimator. For each category
c\in\{1,...,K\}
, we construct the following bias-corrected estimator of
\mu_{c}^{(l)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\widehat{\mu}_{c}^{(l)}=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left[\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j})\cdot X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j}\right]-\frac{1}{n_{l}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{l}}\widehat{\omega}^{(l)}(X_{i}^{(l)})\cdot\left({\bf 1}(Y_{i}^{(l)}=c)-\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{c}(X_{i}^{(l)})\right)\cdot X_{i}^{(l)}.

(14)

In Section
B.2
of Appendix, we provide the full details on the construction of the conditional probability estimator
\widehat{f}^{(l)}(\cdot)
and the density ratio estimator
\widehat{\omega}^{(l)}(\cdot)
using the cross-fitting idea.
In the no covariate shift setting, we can simply set
\widehat{\omega}^{(l)}\equiv 1
in the above estimator, which shares the same theoretical property with the estimator in (
9
).
We then stack the above estimators and construct
\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}=\left([\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}_{1}]^{\intercal},[\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}_{2}]^{\intercal},\cdots,[\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}_{K}]^{\intercal}\right)^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}
.

In the following Algorithm
2
, we further apply the Mirror Prox algorithm in Algorithm
1
by using this updated estimator of
\mu^{(l)}.
Since
\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}
is constructed as the DML estimator, we refer to our proposal as the Mirror Prox Algorithm with DML.

Data:

Source data
\{(X_{i}^{(l)},Y_{i}^{(l)})\}_{i\in[n_{l}]}
for
l\in[L]
; target covariates
\{X_{j}^{\mathbb{Q}}\}_{j\in[N]}
; learning rates for Mirror Prox:
\eta_{\theta},\eta_{\gamma}
; maximum iteration
T
, tolerance
{\rm tol}
.

Result:

Mirror Prox estimators
\widehat{\theta}_{T}
,
\widehat{\gamma}_{T}
.

for

l=1,2,...,L
:

do

Construct the cross-fitting conditional probability estimator
\widehat{f}^{(l)}
and the cross-fitting density ratio estimator
\widehat{\omega}^{(l)}
;

Construct the doubly robust estimators
\{\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}_{c}\}_{1\leq c\leq K}
as in (
14
);
end for

Solve empirical CG-DRO (
8
) with the Mirror Prox Algorithm
1
and return
\widehat{\theta}_{T}
,
\widehat{\gamma}_{T}
.

Algorithm 2

Mirror Prox Algorithm with DML

4
Statistical Convergence Rate

In this section, we establish the convergence rate of
\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}
and defer the construction of confidence intervals for
\theta^{*}
to Section
5
.
To facilitate theoretical analysis, we use
n=\min_{1\leq l\leq L}n_{l}
to denote the minimum source sample size. We impose the following conditions:

Condition (A1)
.

There exist positive constants
c_{0},c_{1}>0
and a sequence
\delta_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, such that the estimators
\{\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}\}_{1\leq l\leq L}
satisfy: for any
t\geq c_{0}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{1\leq l\leq L}\|\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}-\mu^{(l)}\|_{2}\geq t\sqrt{d/n}\right)\leq\exp(-c_{1}t^{2})+\delta_{n}.

(15)

This condition requires that, with high probability,
\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}
is an accurate estimator of
\mu^{(l)}
in the order of
\sqrt{d/n}
. In Appendix
B.2
, we verify that the proposed DML estimators
\{\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}\}_{1\leq l\leq L}
, given in (
9
) or (
14
) of Section
3
, satisfy (
15
) under regimes with and without covariate shift. This holds under mild regularity assumptions, along with the following standard condition from the DML literature
(
Chernozhukov et al. 2018
)
: with high probability
1-\delta_{n}
,

\|\widehat{\omega}^{(l)}/\omega^{(l)}-1\|_{\ell_{2}(\mathbb{P}^{(l)})}\cdot\|\widehat{f}^{(l)}-f^{(l)}\|_{\ell_{2}(\mathbb{P}^{(l)})}=o(n^{-1/2}),

(16)

where
\|\cdot\|_{\ell_{2}(\mathbb{P}^{(l)})}
denotes
\ell_{2}
norm with respect to
\mathbb{P}^{(l)}
using out-of-sample data. In (
15
), we require
t\geq c_{0}
for some positive constant
c_{0}
to account for the scale of
\mathbb{E}[\|\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}-\mu^{(l)}\|_{2}]
, which itself is of order
\sqrt{d/n}
.

Condition (A2)
.

The target covariate observation
X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{i}
is almost surely bounded for
1\leq i\leq N
. There exist constants
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}>0
such that
\kappa_{1}\leq\lambda_{\min}\left(\mathbb{E}[X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{i}(X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{i})^{\intercal}]\right)\leq\lambda_{\max}\left(\mathbb{E}[X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{i}(X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{i})^{\intercal}]\right)\leq\kappa_{2}
. The population CG-DRO model
\theta^{*}
in (
6
) exists and satisfies
\|\theta^{*}\|_{1}\leq C
for some constant
C>0
. The sample sizes
n,N
and the dimension
d
satisfy
n\gg d^{3}
and
N\gg n\log N.

The boundedness condition of the covariate
X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{i}
and
\|\theta^{*}\|_{1}
is mainly imposed to ensure that the conditional probabilities
\frac{\exp(\theta^{*\intercal}_{c}X_{i}^{\mathbb{Q}})}{1+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\exp(\theta^{*\intercal}_{k}X_{i}^{\mathbb{Q}})}
are bounded away from
0
and
1
for each category
c\in\{1,...,K\}
. Such boundedness conditions were adopted to facilitate the theoretical analysis of the binary and multi-class classification models
(
van de Geer et al. 2014
;
Athey et al. 2018
;
Guo et al. 2021
)
. Moreover, the boundedness of the eigenvalues for
\mathbb{E}[X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{i}(X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{i})^{\intercal}]
guarantees the strong convexity and local smoothness of the function
S(\theta)
defined in (
7
) in the neighborhood of
\theta^{*}
.
Lastly, we consider the growing-dimensional regime and assume that
n\gg d^{3}
. We assume
N\gg n\log N
, that is, the target domain has a large amount of unlabeled data compared to the amount of labeled data in the source domains.
Such a regime is common in the unsupervised domain adaptation as the unlabeled data are easier to access in many applications.

In the following proposition, we establish an initial convergence rate of
\widehat{\theta}
. In Sections
4.1
and
4.2
, we derive a more refined rate using more sophisticated analytical tools.

Proposition 1
.

Suppose that
Conditions
(A1)
and
(A2)
hold. Then there exist positive constants
c_{0},c_{1}>0
such that with probability at least
1-N^{-c_{1}d}-\exp(-c_{1}t^{2})-\delta_{n},

\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{t}\cdot(d/n)^{1/4},

(17)

where
t
is a positive value satisfying
c_{0}\leq t\lesssim\sqrt{n/d^{3}}

and both
c_{0}
and the vanishing sequence
\delta_{n}\to 0
are specified in
Condition
(A1)
.

We have explained the reason for requiring
t\geq c_{0}
after stating Condition
(A1)
. The reason for requiring
t\lesssim\sqrt{n/d^{3}}
is to ensure that the
\ell_{1}
norm error
\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{1}
remains bounded with a high probability. Throughout the paper, we shall require
t
to satisfy

c_{0}\leq t\lesssim\sqrt{n/d^{3}}\quad\text{with }c_{0}\text{ specified in Condition \ref{cond:A1}.}

(18)

Importantly, under the assumption
n\gg d^{3}
of Condition
(A2)
, the positive value
t
can still take a wide range of values. For instance, one may choose
t
as a large positive constant or allow it to grow slowly with
n
, such as
t=\sqrt{\log n}
, both of which satisfy (
18
).

The above proposition establishes a slow convergence rate for
\widehat{\theta}
of order
n^{-1/4}
. This suboptimal rate arises because the inner maximization problem within (
6
) and (
8
) is concave but not strongly concave, allowing the existence of multiple maximizers. As a result, quantifying the distance between the sets of maximizers for empirical and population problems leads to a slow rate. A similar phenomenon is observed in
Zhang et al. 2024a
;
Wang et al. 2024
. In the following Theorem
4
, we derive a faster convergence rate of order
n^{-1/2}
through a more refined analysis. The key idea is to construct an approximate optimization problem whose dual problem is strongly concave in
\gamma
.

We highlight that existing M-estimation techniques cannot be applied to achieve this fast convergence rate in our setting.
For M-estimation, one typically replaces a population mean with its empirical counterpart. In contrast, we take an extra maximization over the weight after replacing the population quantity with the corresponding sample average, which introduces additional challenges in controlling the statistical error. Moreover, as the maximization is taken after the expectation, standard M-estimation techniques for non-smooth loss functions, such as least absolute deviation (LAD) minimization discussed in Chapter 5 of
van der Vaart 1998
, cannot be directly applied in our setting.

4.1
Main Idea: Faster Convergence Rate via Risk Approximation

The essential idea of establishing fast convergence is to identify an approximate optimization problem that serves as a surrogate for the original minimax optimization problem. We utilize this approximate optimization problem as a bridge to establish the convergence rate of the original problem. We now introduce the approximate optimization problems and describe the main proof idea. Particularly, we approximate
S(\theta)
in the definition of the objective function in (
6
) with its second-order Taylor expansion near
\widehat{\theta}
, defined as

Q(\theta)=S(\widehat{\theta})+\langle\nabla S(\widehat{\theta}),\theta-\widehat{\theta}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\widehat{\theta})^{\intercal}H(\widehat{\theta})(\theta-\widehat{\theta}),

(19)

where
\nabla S(\theta)
and
H(\theta)
respectively denote the gradient and Hessian of
S(\theta).
We then approximate the original population problem in (
6
) by replacing
S(\theta)
with
Q(\theta)
and define
{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}
as the solution to the following approximate problem:

{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)\quad\text{with}\quad\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)\coloneqq\sum_{l=1}^{L}\gamma_{l}\cdot\theta^{\intercal}\mu^{(l)}+Q(\theta).

(20)

The objective function
\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
can be viewed as a quadratic approximation of
\phi(\theta,\gamma)
and the optimizer
{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}
serves a surrogate of
\theta^{*}
for the theoretical analysis.

Similarly, we define the approximate problem for the original empirical problem in (
8
):

\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)\quad\text{with}\quad\widehat{\phi}_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)\coloneqq\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\gamma}_{l}\cdot\theta^{\intercal}\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}+\widehat{Q}(\theta),

(21)

where
\widehat{Q}(\theta)\coloneqq\widehat{S}(\widehat{\theta})+\langle\nabla\widehat{S}(\widehat{\theta}),\theta-\widehat{\theta}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\widehat{\theta})^{\intercal}\widehat{H}(\widehat{\theta})(\theta-\widehat{\theta})
and
\nabla\widehat{S}(\theta)
and
\widehat{H}(\theta)
denote the gradient and Hessian of
\widehat{S}(\theta)
. In (
21
), we substitute the objective
\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma)
in the original empirical problem (
8
) by
\widehat{\phi}_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
, with
\widehat{Q}(\theta)
serving as a quadratic approximation of
\widehat{S}(\theta).
As a remark,
{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}
are mainly theoretical intermediate quantities used for analysis; we do not implement the approximate problems defined in (
20
) and (
21
).

The following theorem quantifies the error between the solutions of the approximate and original minimax optimization problems. This approximation analysis plays a key role in justifying our surrogate formulation and may be of independent theoretical interest. The full proof is provided in Appendix
A.3
.

Theorem 1
.

Suppose that
Conditions
(A1)
and
(A2)
hold. Then there exists a positive constant
c_{1}>0
such that with probability at least
1-N^{-c_{1}d}-\exp(-c_{1}t^{2})-\delta_{n}
,

\|{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim t\sqrt{{d}/{n}},

(22)

and

\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}=\widehat{\theta},

(23)

where
t>0
satisfies (
18
) and the sequence
\delta_{n}\to 0
is specified in
Condition
(A1)
.

In the above theorem, the approximation error of
{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}
satisfies
\|{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-\theta^{*}\|\lesssim\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|^{2}
, indicating that it scales quadratically with the estimation error of
\widehat{\theta}
.
Together with Proposition
1
, we achieve a fast parametric convergence rate for
\|{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}
. Importantly, we show that
\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}=\widehat{\theta}
, indicating that the approximate empirical problem in (
21
) shares the same solution as the original empirical problem in (
8
).

We now demonstrate our proof idea for the fast rate of
\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}
, via the following triangle inequality in Figure
3
:

\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq\|\widehat{\theta}-\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}+\|\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}-{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}+\|{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-\theta^{*}\|_{2},

where
\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}
and
\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}
are the solutions for two approximate problems linking
\widehat{\theta}
and
\theta^{*}
, as defined in (
21
) and (
20
), respectively.
Theorem
1
establishes that the first and third terms on the right-hand side converge at a fast rate, while the following Theorem
3
further establishes that
\|\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}-\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}
also converges at a fast rate.

Figure 3
:
Illustration of the proof strategy for the fast rate of
\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}
. Theorem
1
establishes the approximation errors of
\|\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}
and
\|\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}-\widehat{\theta}\|_{2}
, while the following Theorem
3
establishes the convergence rate of
\|\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}-\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}
.

4.2
Fast Convergence Rate of the Approximate Minimax Optimization

In this section, we establish the convergence rate for
\|\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}-\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}
.
Our main proof strategy is to derive alternative representations for both
\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}
and

\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}
by analyzing their corresponding dual problems.
Specifically, we compute the associated optimal weights and then express
\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}
as solutions to standard minimization problems
parameterized by these optimal weights. We primarily focus on the approximate population minimax optimization problem in (
20
), while similar arguments extend to the empirical version in (
21
). In particular, we interchange the max and min operations in (
20
), which yields the following reformulation:

\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\left[\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)\right]=\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\left[\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)\right].

The right-hand side of the above equation provides an alternative way of identifying
\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}
:

•

Step 1.
We compute the maximizer for
\gamma
, referred to as the optimal weight, by solving

\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}=\mathop{\arg\max}_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}F(\gamma)\quad\text{with}\quad F(\gamma)\coloneqq\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma).

(24)

•

Step 2.
With the optimal weight
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap},
we solve the following minimization problem,

\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}\coloneqq\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\ \phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}).

(25)

The benefit of (
24
) and (
25
) is that both objective functions
F(\gamma)
and
\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap})
admit a quadratic form, which facilitates analytical tractability and enables sharper theoretical characterization. We provide a rigorous justification for identifying
\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}
using (
24
) and (
25
) in the following Theorem
2
.

To facilitate the discussion, we define the matrices

\mathcal{U}=(\mu^{(1)},\mu^{(2)},...,\mu^{(L)})\in\mathbb{R}^{dK\times L}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\mathcal{U}}=\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\mu}^{(1)},\widehat{\mu}^{(2)},...,\widehat{\mu}^{(L)}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{dK\times L},

(26)

where each
\mu^{(l)}\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}
is defined in (
7
) and
\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}
is the estimator of
\mu^{(l)}
. Our analysis requires the vectors
\{\mu^{(l)}\}_{l\in[L]}
to be linearly independent, stated in the following assumption.

Condition (A3)
.

The
L
-th largest singular value of
\mathcal{U}
is positive, i.e.,
\sigma_{L}(\mathcal{U})>0
.

The above condition holds as long as the vectors
\{\mu^{(l)}\}_{l\in[L]}
are not collinear. When there exist distributional shifts across distributions, the linear independence assumption among
\{\mu^{(l)}\}_{l\in[L]}
tends to hold plausibly. However, when two source distributions are identical, their corresponding means
their corresponding
\mu^{(l)}

also coincide, violating Condition
(A3)
. This highlights that some degree of heterogeneity across environments is necessary for Condition
(A3)
to hold. Such heterogeneity is common in practice, supporting the applicability of our theoretical analysis. We also emphasize that our theoretical analysis does not assume
\sigma_{L}(\mathcal{U})
to be a fixed constant; instead, we allow it to decay with the sample size
n
, reflecting scenarios with increasing collinearity as more data are collected.

In the following theorem, we establish equivalent expressions of
{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}.

Theorem 2
.

Suppose that
Conditions
(A1)
–
(A3)
hold.
{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}
defined in (
20
) admits the following form,

{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}=\widehat{\theta}-[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\left(\mathcal{U}\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}+\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})\right)\quad\text{with}\quad{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap}=\mathop{\arg\max}_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}F(\gamma),

(27)

where
F(\gamma)
is defined in (
24
). Similarly, there exists a positive constant
c_{1}>0
such that with probability larger than
1-N^{-c_{1}d}-e^{-c_{1}t^{2}}-\delta_{n}
,

\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}=\widehat{\theta}-[\widehat{H}(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}\widehat{\gamma}_{\rm ap}+\nabla\widehat{S}(\widehat{\theta})\right)\quad\text{with}\quad\widehat{\gamma}_{\rm ap}=\mathop{\arg\max}_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{F}(\gamma),

(28)

where
\widehat{F}(\gamma)=\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\widehat{\phi}_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
with
\widehat{\phi}_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
defined in (
21
),
t>0
satisfies (
18
), and the sequence
\delta_{n}\to 0
is specified in
Condition
(A1)
.

Theorem
2
leads to two key insights. We focus primarily on (
27
), as it naturally extends to the formulation in (
28
). First, the expression in (
27
) provides an explicit solution for
{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}
, which arises because the unconstrained quadratic optimization problem in (
25
) admits a closed-form minimizer. Second, we also obtain a closed-form expression for
F(\gamma)
, since it corresponds to the minimum value of a quadratic objective function as described in (
24
),

\displaystyle F(\gamma)=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{U}\gamma+\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})\right)^{\intercal}[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\left(\mathcal{U}\gamma+\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})\right)+\widehat{\theta}^{\intercal}\mathcal{U}\gamma.

(29)

We emphasize that the definitions of
\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
and
F(\gamma)
depend on the data-driven estimator
\widehat{\theta}
, and consequently, so do the optimizers
({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap},\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap})
. Therefore, despite the use of a star in their notations,
{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}
and
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
are not population-level quantities, but are instead data-dependent intermediate quantities to facilitate the theoretical analysis.

We now explain why Theorem
2
leads to a fast convergence rate for
\|\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}-{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}
. The main idea is that both
\widehat{F}(\gamma)
and
{F}(\gamma)
are smooth quadratic functions of
\gamma
. When these two functions are close, their minimizers,
\widehat{\gamma}_{\rm ap}
and
{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap}
, are also close. Given the explicit formulas for
\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}
and
{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}
in this theorem, the closeness of the weights directly implies the closeness of the estimators, yielding the fast convergence rate for
\|\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}-{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}
.

Theorem 3
.

Suppose that
Conditions
(A1)
–
(A3)
hold. Then there exists a positive constant
c_{1}>0
such that with probability at least
1-N^{-c_{1}d}-\exp(-c_{1}t^{2})-\delta_{n},

\|\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}-\theta_{\rm ap}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{t}{\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})}\left(1+\|\nabla S(\theta^{*})\|_{2}+\|\mathcal{U}\|_{2}+\|\mathcal{U}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\sqrt{d/n},

(30)

where
t>0
satisfies (
18
) and the sequence
\delta_{n}\to 0
is specified in
Condition
(A1)
.

As illustrated in Figure
3
, we combine the above theorem and Theorem
1
to establish the fast convergence rate of
\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}.
We impose the following condition to further simplify the final upper bound established.

Condition (A4)
.

There exists a constant
C_{0}>0
such that

\max\{\|\mathcal{U}\|_{2},\|\nabla S(\theta^{*})\|_{2}\}\leq C_{0}
.

Theorem 4
.

Suppose that
Conditions
(A1)
–
(A4)
hold. Then there exist positive constants
c_{1},C>0
such that with probability at least
1-N^{-c_{1}d}-\exp(-c_{1}t^{2})-\delta_{n},

\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq\tau\sqrt{d/n}\quad\text{with}\quad\tau:=C\left(1+\frac{1}{\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})}\right)t,

(31)

where
t>0
satisfies (
18
) and the sequence
\delta_{n}\to 0
is specified in
Condition
(A1)
.

This theorem establishes an upper bound on
\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}
, showing that
\widehat{\theta}
converges to
\theta^{*}
at the rate
\sqrt{d/n}
, up to a multiplicative factor
\tau
. The factor
\tau
remains of constant order if
\sigma_{L}(\mathcal{U})
is bounded away from zero. Compared to the slower rate established in Proposition
1
, our refined analysis, based on constructing two approximate optimization problems, yields a significantly sharper rate. Notably, this rate matches the optimal rate for estimating regression parameters in the single source setting
(
Wainwright 2019
)
.

5
Uncertainty Quantification for Minimax Optimization

In this section, we focus on statistical inference for the CG-DRO model
\theta^{*}
in (
3
), interpreted as a generalizable model that captures stable predictive patterns across diverse domains. Our goal is to construct the confidence interval for each component
\theta^{*}_{j}
with
1\leq j\leq dK
, or to test hypotheses of the form
H_{j}:\theta^{*}_{j}=0
. When
\theta^{*}_{j}\neq 0
, we say that the
j
-th element is distributionally robust significant, meaning that it is likely to exert a consistent and non-negligible effect even under distribution shifts. For clarity, we focus on the inference for
\theta^{*}_{1}
, though the proposed methodology naturally extends to any
\theta^{*}_{j}
with
1\leq j\leq dK
.

In Section
5.1
, we demonstrate the challenge of inference for
\theta^{*}_{1}
, that is, the empirical distribution of
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
in (
8
) may be nonstandard. Throughout this paper, we use the terms
nonnormal
and
nonstandard
interchangeably to indicate that the limiting distribution of the estimator is not Gaussian. The standard inference approach based on taking quantiles of the Gaussian distribution cannot be applied to such a nonstandard inference problem. In Sections
5.2
and
5.3
, we propose novel inference procedures to address this challenge.

5.1
Nonnormal Limiting Distribution of the Data-driven Estimator

We demonstrate in the following that the empirical distribution of
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
is not asymptotically normal. We consider a simplified setting with
L=2
sources and
K=1
(binary classification), where each source contains
n_{l}=400
samples and the covariate dimension is
d=20
. The number of unlabeled target samples is fixed at
N=4000
. We conduct 500 independent simulations, with further details provided in Appendix
E.1
. Figure
4
reports the empirical distributions of the first entry
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
and the weight of the first source
\widehat{\gamma}_{1}
, where
\widehat{\theta}
and
\widehat{\gamma}
are computed using Algorithm
2
. As shown in the left and middle panels of Figure
4
, the empirical distribution of
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
deviates from normality and is not centered at the true value
\theta^{*}_{1}
.

Figure 4
:
Empirical distributions of
\widehat{\gamma}_{1}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
in the nonregular, unstable, and regular settings. The top row corresponds to the first source’s estimated weight
\widehat{\gamma}_{1}
, while the bottom row corresponds to the estimated
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
. Vertical red lines indicate true parameter values, while dashed green lines show the empirical average across
500
simulations. Blue histograms with overlaid kernel density estimates depict the empirical distributions of estimates across 500 simulations. The exact simulation settings are reported in Appendix
E.1
.

We provide insight into the emergence of the nonnormal
limiting distribution reported in Figure
4
. To highlight the core idea, we
informally assume that the minimax problem allows for the exchange of the max and
min operations, and write the empirical problem
(
8
) as

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\widehat{\gamma})\quad\text{with}\quad\widehat{\gamma}\in\mathop{\arg\max}_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\left[\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma)\right].

(32)

The above expression describes a dual method of solving the empirical CG-DRO problem, where we first solve for the optimal weight
\widehat{\gamma}
by exchanging the max
and min operations, and then minimize the objective

\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\widehat{\gamma})
with respect to
\theta
. Under
regularity conditions, for a fixed
\gamma\in\Delta^{L}
, the standard
M-estimation theory implies that

\widehat{\theta}(\gamma)\coloneqq\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma)

is asymptotically normal. However, as demonstrated in Figure
4
, the estimated
weight
\widehat{\gamma}
in (
32
) is data-driven and
may follow a nonstandard limiting distribution. This, in turn, induces a nonnormal limiting distribution for
\widehat{\theta}
.

We now describe two commonly encountered data-generating mechanisms leading to nonnormal limiting distributions in Figure
4
. Particularly, we specify the source-specific conditional
probabilities as follows, with coefficients

\beta^{(1)},\beta^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\mathbb{P}^{(l)}(Y=1|X)=\left[1+\exp(-X^{\intercal}\beta^{(l)})\right]^{-1},\;\textrm{for}\;\;1\leq l\leq 2.

•

Scenario 1: sparse shifts between
\beta^{(1)}
and
\beta^{(2)}
.
When

\beta^{(1)}
differs significantly from
\beta^{(2)}
in only a few entries, the minimax optimization problem tends to put more weight on the source domain with smaller magnitude of
\|\beta^{(l)}\|_{2}.
Consequently, most of
\widehat{\gamma}_{1}
out of
500 simulations lie near the boundary
0
, which is reported in the left panel of Figure

4
. The non-negative constraint
induced by the simplex
\Delta^{L}
leads to a mixture distribution of

\widehat{\gamma}_{1}
and induces substantial bias in

\widehat{\theta}_{1}
and a nonnormal distribution of
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
.

•

Scenario 2: similar
\beta^{(1)}
and
\beta^{(2)}
.

In this regime, all entries of
\beta^{(1)}
are similar to the
corresponding entries of
\beta^{(2)}
, indicating the existence of a
weak shift. This causes collinearity among
\{{\mu}^{(l)}\}_{1\leq l\leq L}
,
resulting in the instability in calculating the data-driven weight

\widehat{\gamma}
. As reported in the middle panel of Figure

4
,
\widehat{\gamma}_{1}
fluctuates widely
between
0
and
1
, leading again to a nonnormal empirical distribution
for
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
.

The above two scenarios showcase the difficulty of identifying a limiting distribution of
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
when there exist certain similarity patterns between the two source sites.
More generally,
Scenario 1
is a primary example of a

nonregular
regime, which refers to estimators having a
nonnormal distribution due to the boundary constraint
(
Self and Liang 1987
;
Andrews 1999
;
Drton 2009
)
, i.e., the simplex
constraint in our problem.
Scenario 2
is an example of an

unstable
regime, referring to estimators having a nonnormal
limiting distribution due to the instability of the system, i.e., the
collinearity among
\{{\mu}^{(l)}\}_{l\in[L]}
. Moreover, there are scenarios where the limiting distribution of

\widehat{\theta}
is normal, and we refer to this regime as the

regular
regime. An example of the regular regime is that

\beta^{(1)}
and
\beta^{(2)}
are very different, as shown in the rightmost panel of Figure
4
.

5.2
Main Idea of Perturbation and Filtering

We present the main idea of constructing a uniformly valid confidence interval for
\theta^{*}_{1}
, ensuring coverage even when the estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
does not follow an asymptotic normal distribution, as discussed in Section
5.1
. We begin by outlining the key intuition behind our proposed perturbation methods. This intuition is based on a dual characterization of
\theta^{*}
: in the following Section
6.1
, we identify a specific optimal population weight
\gamma^{*}\in\Delta^{L}
, defined in (
41
), and Proposition
2
shows that
\theta^{*}
is expressed as the solution to the following minimization problem,

\theta^{*}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\ \phi(\theta,\gamma^{*}).

(33)

If granted the access to the true
\gamma^{*}
(though in practice unknown), we apply the standard M-estimation theory to establish the asymptotic normality of the semi-oracle global minimizer defined as
\widehat{\theta}(\gamma^{*})\coloneqq\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma^{*}).
We then construct the confidence interval by computing the quantiles associated with the normal distributions of
\widehat{\theta}(\gamma^{*})
. However, since
\gamma^{*}
is unknown, we generate a collection of perturbed weight vectors around
\widehat{\gamma}
, defined in (
32
), and show that our particular perturbation scheme ensures that at least one of the perturbed weights lies close to
\gamma^{*}
. These perturbed weights are then used to construct a valid confidence interval, as one of them effectively mimics the true but unknown
\gamma^{*}
. Intuitively, this procedure separates the overall uncertainty into two components: the uncertainty arising from estimating
\gamma^{*}
, and the uncertainty in estimating
\theta^{*}
conditional on knowing
\gamma^{*}
.

In the remaining of this subsection, we describe the perturbation idea and explain why one of the perturbed weight vectors nearly recovers
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
, defined in (
24
). We then establish that
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
is sufficiently close to
\gamma^{*}
and hence one of the perturbed weight vectors also nearly recovers
\gamma^{*}
. The overall idea is illustrated in Figure
5
. There are two reasons that we do not directly approximate
\gamma^{*}
but build the approximation via
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
: firstly, as shown in the following (
41
), the definition of
\gamma^{*}
depends on a local neighborhood around
\theta^{*}
, which makes it difficult to estimate directly from the data; secondly, it is relatively easier to approximate
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
since we can directly approximate the objective function
F(\gamma)
in (
29
) that is used to compute
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
.

Figure 5
:
Demonstration of the perturbation idea. We generate a collection of
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}
for
1\leq m\leq M
such that one of them is nearly equal to
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap},
which is itself close to
\gamma^{*}
.

We explain in the following how to design the perturbation method to approximate
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}.
We recall the definition of
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}=\mathop{\arg\max}_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}F(\gamma)
, where the objective
F(\gamma)
is given in (
29
). Therefore, to accurately approximate
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
, it suffices to construct an accurate approximation of the objective
F(\gamma)
. A natural candidate is the following plug-in quantity
\widehat{F}(\gamma)
, which substitutes the true matrix
\mathcal{U}
in (
29
) with its empirical counterpart
\widehat{\mathcal{U}}
:

\widehat{F}(\gamma)=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}\gamma+\nabla\widehat{S}(\widehat{\theta})\right)^{\intercal}[\widehat{H}(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}\gamma+\nabla\widehat{S}(\widehat{\theta})\right)+\widehat{\theta}^{\intercal}\widehat{\mathcal{U}}\gamma,\quad\text{with}\quad\widehat{\mathcal{U}}=\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\mu}^{(1)},\dots,\widehat{\mu}^{(L)}\end{pmatrix}.

However, due to the estimation error
\|\widehat{\mathcal{U}}-\mathcal{U}\|_{2}
, directly computing
\mathop{\arg\min}_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{F}(\gamma)
does not yield an approximation of
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
that is accurate enough for the inferential purpose. To address this issue, we introduce a perturbation strategy that leverages the limiting distribution of
\widehat{\mathcal{U}}
. Specifically, as shown in Appendix
B.3
, we establish the following asymptotic normality result:

\left[\widehat{\bf V}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right]^{-1/2}\left(\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}-\mu^{(l)}\right)\xlongrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,{\bf I}_{dK}),\quad\textrm{for $1\leq l\leq L$,}

where
\widehat{\bf V}_{\mu}^{(l)}
is an estimated covariance matrix for
\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}
as defined in Appendix
B.3
. We condition on the observed data and generate
M
independent matrices
\{\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{[m]}\}_{1\leq m\leq M}
, each constructed as follows:

\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{[m]}=\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\mu}^{(1,m)},\dots,\widehat{\mu}^{(L,m)}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{with}\quad\widehat{\mu}^{(l,m)}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\widehat{\mu}^{(l)},\widehat{\bf V}^{(l)}_{\mu}\right)\quad\text{for }1\leq l\leq L.

For each matrix
\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{[m]}
, we define a perturbed objective function:

\widehat{F}^{[m]}(\gamma)\coloneqq-\frac{1}{2}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{[m]}\gamma+\nabla\widehat{S}(\widehat{\theta})\right)^{\intercal}[\widehat{H}(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{[m]}\gamma+\nabla\widehat{S}(\widehat{\theta})\right)+\widehat{\theta}^{\intercal}\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{[m]}\gamma,

and obtain the perturbed weight vector
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}
by solving

\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}=\mathop{\arg\max}_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{F}^{[m]}(\gamma).

(34)

We create the perturbation by resampling the key component of the objective function.
In particular, we perturb the above optimization problem by resampling
\widehat{\mathcal{U}}
,
which is used in computing
\widehat{\gamma}
, thereby generating a collection of
perturbed optimization problems as described in (
34
). The main idea of this construction is that, with high probability, there exists some
realization
\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{[m]}
that is sufficiently close to the true
\mathcal{U}
,
implying that the corresponding perturbed objective
\widehat{F}^{[m]}(\gamma)

is close to the population-level objective
F(\gamma)
. Consequently, the maximizer

\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}
is close to
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
, as illustrated in
Figure
5
.

To formalize this intuition, we establish in Theorem
6
that,
for a sufficiently large resampling size
M
, there exists some index
m^{*}
such that
