\|\widehat{\gamma}^{[m^{*}]}-\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{{\rm err}(M)}{\sqrt{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{d\log N}{N}},\quad\text{with}\quad\lim_{M\to\infty}{\rm err}_{n}(M)=0.

(35)

This means that with sufficiently many resampling, there exists
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m^{*}]}
closely recovering
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
, which is itself nearly identical to the population weight
\gamma^{*}
. As a result, if we knew this particular index
m^{*}
(but we do not know when implementing the method), it allows us to perform inference as if we knew
\gamma^{*}
.

Filtering.
Instead of using all perturbed weights
\{\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}\}_{m\in[M]}
defined in (
34
), we construct a filtered index set
\mathcal{M}\subseteq[M]
to exclude those extreme perturbations and control the randomness.
As discussed in Section
5.2
, our goal is to obtain a resampled
\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{[m]}
that is sufficiently close to the true
\mathcal{U}
. Based on our perturbation mechanism, an ideal resample
\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{[m]}
should not deviate significantly from
\widehat{\mathcal{U}}
. This motivates filtering out extreme resamples that fall in the tails of the
\widehat{\mathcal{U}}
’s distribution. In particular, for a prespecified significance level
\alpha_{0}\in(0,0.01]
, we define the filtered index set
\mathcal{M}
as follows:

\mathcal{M}:=\left\{1\leq m\leq M:\max_{1\leq l\leq L}\max_{1\leq j\leq dK}\frac{\left|\widehat{\mu}^{(l,m)}_{j}-\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}_{j}\right|}{\sqrt{[\widehat{\bf V}^{(l)}_{\mu}]_{j,j}+1/n_{l}}}\leq(1+\eta_{0})\cdot z_{\alpha_{0}/(dKL)}\right\},

(36)

where
\eta_{0}>0
is a small positive constant to account for finite-sample variability (with a default value of
0.1
). The construction of
\mathcal{M}
achieves two purposes simultaneously: (1) the index set excludes extreme resamples far away from
\widehat{\mathcal{U}}
; (2)
\mathcal{M}
contains at least one index
m^{*}
such that the corresponding
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m^{*}]}
lies sufficiently close to
\gamma^{*}
.

5.3
Inferential Procedure

We now provide the full details for constructing the confidence interval (CI) for
\theta^{*}_{1}
.
As implied by the expression of
\theta^{*}
in (
33
), the semi-oracle estimator
\widehat{\theta}(\gamma^{*})\coloneqq\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma^{*})
is asymptotically normal and centered on
\theta^{*}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta}\phi(\theta,\gamma^{*})
. As discussed in Section
5.2
, we know that there exists
m^{*}\in\mathcal{M}
such that
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m^{*}]}\approx\gamma^{*}
. Intuitively, this implies that the limiting distribution of
\widehat{\theta}(\widehat{\gamma}^{[m^{*}]})\coloneqq\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\widehat{\gamma}^{[m^{*}]})
is Gaussian, enabling us to construct the confidence interval for
\theta^{*}_{1}
using the quantiles of the limiting distribution of
\widehat{\theta}(\widehat{\gamma}^{[m^{*}]})
. However, since the exact identity of the index
m^{*}
is unknown in practice, we construct the individual interval for each
m\in\mathcal{M}
and take their union to form the final confidence interval.

For each
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}
constructed via (
34
), we construct a perturbed estimator of
\theta^{*}
as

\widehat{\theta}^{[m]}:=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]})=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}_{l}\cdot\theta^{\intercal}\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}+\widehat{S}(\theta).

(37)

When
N\gg\max\{n_{1},\cdots,n_{L}\}
, the error of
\widehat{\theta}^{[m]}
can be approximately decomposed as:

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta}^{[m]}-\theta^{*}\approx-\left[H(\theta^{*})\right]^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\left(\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}_{l}-{\gamma}^{*}_{l}\right){\mu}^{(l)}-\left[H(\theta^{*})\right]^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\gamma}^{*}_{l}\left(\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}-{\mu}^{(l)}\right),

(38)

where
H(\theta^{*})
denotes the Hessian matrix of
S(\theta^{*})
; see the exact statement in Proposition
5
of Appendix.
In the above decomposition, the first term on the right-hand side represents the uncertainty of estimating
\gamma^{*}
with a perturbed weight vector
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}
, while the second term captures the uncertainty of estimating
\mu^{(l)}
. As illustrated in Figure
5
, our perturbation strategy ensures that there exists at least one
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}
that is nearly the same as
\gamma^{*}
. Consequently,
we mainly quantify the uncertainty arising from
-\left[H(\theta^{*})\right]^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\gamma}^{*}_{l}\left(\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}-{\mu}^{(l)}\right)
.
Given a pre-specified significance level
\alpha
such that
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
, we define
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha-\alpha_{0}
and construct

{\rm Int}^{[m]}=\left(\widehat{\theta}^{[m]}_{1}-z_{\alpha^{\prime}/2}\sqrt{\widehat{\bf V}_{1,1}^{[m]}},\widehat{\theta}^{[m]}_{1}+z_{\alpha^{\prime}/2}\sqrt{\widehat{\bf V}_{1,1}^{[m]}}\right),

(39)

where
z_{\alpha^{\prime}/2}
denotes the upper
\alpha^{\prime}/2
quantile of the standard normal distribution, and
\widehat{\bf V}^{[m]}\in\mathbb{R}^{dK\times dK}
is the estimator of the covariance matrix for
-\left[H(\theta^{*})\right]^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\gamma}^{*}_{l}\left(\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}-{\mu}^{(l)}\right)
, using the perturbed quantities
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}
,
\widehat{\theta}^{[m]}
. We provide the explicit form of
\widehat{\bf V}^{[m]}
in Appendix
B.4.2
.

Since we do not know the index
m^{*}
, we take a union of the perturbed intervals for
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

{\rm CI}=\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}{\rm Int}^{[m]}.

(40)

Regarding a level-
\alpha
test of
H_{0}:\theta^{*}_{1}=0
, we shall reject
H_{0}
if
0\not\in{\rm CI}
. We illustrate the proposed perturbation-based inferential approach in Figure
6
.

Figure 6
:
The top panel illustrates the construction of
{\rm Int}^{[m]}
,
and the bottom panel illustrates our final confidence interval is taken as a union of
{\rm Int}^{[m]}
over
m\in\mathcal{M}
.

The validity of the proposed CI in (
40
) requires the resampling size
M
to be sufficiently large. In practice, we typically choose
M=500
or
M=1000
, which achieves uniform coverage in the experiments presented in Section
7
. Moreover, we observe that the length of the resulting CI is insensitive to the choice of the resampling size
M
.

We now comment on the computational cost of the proposed inferential procedure.
For each index
m\in\mathcal{M}
, the weight vector
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}
is computed by solving the strongly convex problem in (
34
). Given
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}
, we then compute the estimator
\widehat{\theta}^{[m]}
in (
37
). Thus, the computation of the perturbed estimator
\widehat{\theta}^{[m]}
requires the solution of only two convex problems, both of which can be solved computationally efficiently. Moreover, in practice, we further accelerate the entire procedure by parallelizing the computations across
M
independent perturbations. For example, in our simulation experiments under setting
(S4)
in Section
7
, the inferential procedure takes approximately 20 seconds in total with
M=500
. Compared to the methods that require repeated sampling and retraining based on the original data, our method avoids solving machine learning models repeatedly when constructing the DML estimators of
{\mathcal{U}}
.
This difference results in improvements in both computational and memory efficiency.

Remark 2

(Inference for Minimax Problem)
.

We address the nonstandard inference through decoupling the origins of the randomness into two components: the nonstandard limiting distribution of the weight estimator
\widehat{\gamma}
and the standard limiting distribution of
\widehat{\theta}(\gamma^{*}):=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma^{*})
. We devise a novel perturbation approach to handle the nonstandard component
\widehat{\gamma}
while using the Gaussian quantile to track the standard component
\widehat{\theta}(\gamma^{*})
.
Although
Guo 2023
employed perturbation techniques to address nonregular inference, their method relied on a closed-form solution under squared loss in regression settings, which is hard to generalize to other loss functions. In contrast, we tackle the more challenging classification regime with cross-entropy loss and develop a computationally efficient perturbation-based method by carefully designing a surrogate problem, even when there is no closed-form solution to the original problem. In general, we propose a general and novel inferential tool to handle the challenging nonstandard inference for the minimax optimization problem.

6
Theoretical Justification for Uncertainty Quantification

In this section, we provide a theoretical justification for the perturbation method introduced in Section
5.2
, and analyze the confidence interval constructed in (
40
).
Specifically, in Section
6.1
, we formally define the population weight vector
\gamma^{*}
, and show that the approximate weight
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
, defined in (
29
), lies sufficiently close to it. In Section
6.2
, we establish theoretical guarantees for the proposed confidence interval, including its coverage and length results.

6.1
Definition of the Population Optimal Weight
\gamma^{*}

We have demonstrated in (
35
) that there exists a resampled weight
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m^{*}]}
that is sufficiently close to
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}.
However, such a
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
is not readily used for statistical inference as it depends on the data-driven estimator
\widehat{\theta}
and
\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta}\phi(\theta,\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap})
is not necessarily the same as
\theta^{*}.
To address this,
we now define the population weight vector
\gamma^{*}\in\Delta^{L}
as follows:

\gamma^{*}\in\mathop{\arg\max}_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}F_{\rm loc}^{*}(\gamma)\quad\text{with}\quad F_{\rm loc}^{*}(\gamma)\coloneq\min_{\theta\in\Theta_{\rm loc}}\phi(\theta,\gamma),

(41)

where

\Theta_{\rm loc}=\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq 2\tau\sqrt{d/n}\right\}\quad\text{with}\;\tau\;\text{defined in}\;\eqref{eq: final rate}.

(42)

The local region
\Theta_{\mathrm{loc}}
characterizes a
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{d/n})
neighborhood around
\theta^{*}
, and then
\gamma^{*}
is defined as the optimal solution to the restricted minimax problem in this local region
\Theta_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

We now demonstrate two important properties of the population weight vector
\gamma^{*}
defined in (
41
). First, we establish in the following proposition that
\theta^{*}
is the minimizer of
\phi(\theta,\gamma^{*})
. This implies that if
\gamma^{*}
were known, the minimax optimization problem is reduced to a minimization problem and we can leverage the asymptotic normality of
\widehat{\theta}(\gamma^{*})-\theta^{*}
to construct confidence interval for
\theta^{*}_{1}
, where
\widehat{\theta}(\gamma^{*})\coloneqq\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\ \widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma^{*}).

Proposition 2
.

For any
\gamma^{*}
defined in (
41
), the population CG-DRO model
\theta^{*}
in (
6
) satisfies

\theta^{*}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\Theta_{\rm loc}}\ \phi(\theta,\gamma^{*})=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\ \phi(\theta,\gamma^{*}).

(43)

Second, in Theorem
5
, we establish that
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
is sufficiently close to
\gamma^{*}
. We provide intuition for why the formulation in (
41
), which restricts optimization to the local set
\Theta_{\rm loc}
, ensures proximity of
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
to
\gamma^{*}
. Recall that
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
and
\gamma^{*}
are defined as solutions to dual problems with the objective functions
\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
and
\phi(\theta,\gamma)
, given in (
24
) and (
41
), respectively. The function
\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
is constructed as a second-order approximation to
\phi(\theta,\gamma)
in a neighborhood near
\widehat{\theta}
. This implies that the two objective functions
\phi(\theta,\gamma)
and
\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
are uniformly close to each other for
\theta\in\Theta_{\rm loc}
. This uniform approximation implies that their corresponding solutions,
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
and
\gamma^{*}
, must also be close. We refer to the use of the local set
\Theta_{\rm loc}
, along with the associated analysis in Theorem
1
, as the localization technique. Beyond its role in our theoretical development, this localization approach may be of independent interest for the analysis of empirical minimax problems involving approximate objective functions.

Theorem 5
.

Suppose that
Conditions
(A1)
–
(A4)
hold. Then there exists a positive constant
c_{1}>0
such that with probability at least
1-n^{-c_{1}d}-e^{-c_{1}t^{2}}-\delta_{n}
,

\|{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap}-\gamma^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\tau^{2}(d/n)^{3/4},\quad\textrm{with}\quad\tau:=C\left(1+\frac{1}{\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})}\right)t.

where the value
t
satisfies
c_{0}\leq t\ll\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})(n/d)^{1/4}
, with the constant
c_{0}>0
and the sequence
\delta_{n}\to 0
specified in
Condition
(A1)
.

The above theorem parallels Theorem
1
: while Theorem
1
establishes the closeness between the primal variable
\theta
of the original and approximate minimax optimization problems, Theorem
5
quantifies the approximation error associated with the dual variable
\gamma
.
The condition
c_{0}\leq t\ll\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})(n/d)^{1/4}
implies that
\sigma_{L}(\mathcal{U})\gg(d/n)^{1/8}
, suggesting that
\{\mu^{(l)}\}_{l\in[L]}
cannot be overly collinear. In the special case where
\sigma_{L}(\mathcal{U})
is bounded by a constant, the condition on
t
is satisfied for any
c_{0}\leq t\ll(n/d)^{1/8}
. Similar to the discussion on the range of
t
in (
18
), one may choose
t
as a large positive constant or allow it to grow slowly with
n
, such as
t=\sqrt{\log n}
.

6.2
Justification for Confidence Interval

We now provide the theoretical justification for CI in (
40
). The essential component of the justification is to establish the existence of one resampled weight vector
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m^{*}]}
that nearly recovers
\gamma^{*}
. With this, the remaining proof is based on the decomposition (
38
) and the standard M-estimation inferential theory. For clarity, in this subsection, we focus on the fixed dimension
d
setting, although our analysis can be directly extended to a growing-dimensional regime at the expense of more involved notation. We define
{\rm err}_{n}(M)
as a decreasing function of the resampling size
M
,

{\rm err}_{n}(M):=\left(\frac{1}{2}c^{*}(\alpha_{0},d){\rm Vol}(dK)\right)^{-1/dK}\left(\frac{\log n}{M}\right)^{1/dKL},

(44)

where
\alpha_{0}\in(0,0.01]
is the pre-specified constant used to construct the filtered index set
\mathcal{M}
in (
36
),
c^{*}(\alpha_{0},d)
is a positive value depending on
\alpha_{0}
and
d
(with its explicit form given in Appendix
B.4.4
),
and
{\rm Vol}(dK)
denotes the volume of the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{dK}
.
When the dimension
d
is fixed, both
c^{*}(\alpha_{0},d)
and
{\rm Vol}(dK)
become positive constants independent of the sample size
n
and the resampling size
M
. Consequently,
{\rm err}_{n}(M)
scales as
(\log n/M)^{1/dKL}
, which tends to zero as the resampling size
M
increases.

We now introduce the following limiting distribution condition of
\widehat{\mathcal{U}}=(\widehat{\mu}^{(1)},...,\widehat{\mu}^{(L)})
.

Condition (A5)
.

In the fixed dimension regime,
the estimator
\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}
, for
1\leq l\leq L
, satisfies

\left[{\bf V}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right]^{-1/2}\left(\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}-\mu^{(l)}\right)\xlongrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,{\bf I}_{dK}),\;\;\textrm{and}\;\;\|n_{l}(\widehat{\bf V}_{\mu}^{(l)}-{\bf V}_{\mu}^{(l)})\|_{2}\xlongrightarrow{p}0,

(45)

where
{\bf V}_{\mu}^{(l)}
and
\widehat{\bf V}_{\mu}^{(l)}
denote the population and estimated covariance matrices of
\mu^{(l)}
. Additionally, there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that the scaled covariance matrix satisfies
\|n_{l}{\bf V}_{\mu}^{(l)}\|_{2}\leq c_{1}
, and its diagonal entries are bounded below with
\min_{1\leq j\leq dK}n_{l}[{\bf V}_{\mu}^{(l)}]_{j,j}\geq c_{2}
.

Compared to Condition
(A1)
, this condition additionally requires that each
\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}
admits a valid limiting distribution together with a consistent covariance estimator
\widehat{\bf V}_{\mu}^{(l)}
. In Appendix
B.3
, we provide the explicit expressions of both
{\bf V}_{\mu}^{(l)}
and
\widehat{\bf V}_{\mu}^{(l)}
, and verify that this condition holds for the proposed DML estimators
\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}
in (
9
) and (
14
) under covariate shift and non-shift regimes, respectively.

In the following theorem, we control
\min_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\|{\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}}-\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}.

Theorem 6
.

Suppose that
Conditions
(A1)
–
(A5)
hold. Then

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\liminf_{M\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\left\{\min_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\|{\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}}-\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})}\cdot\left(\frac{{\rm err}_{n}(M)}{\sqrt{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{N}}\right)\right\}\geq 1-\alpha_{0},

where
\alpha_{0}\in(0,0.01]
is the pre-specified constant used to construct
\mathcal{M}
in (
36
).

Note that the error term
{\rm err}_{n}(M)
vanishes as the resampling size
M
increases, indicating that the best resampled discrepancy becomes the order of
o(n^{-1/2})
for a sufficiently large
M
, if
\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})\gg\sqrt{n\log N/N}
.
The pre-specified constant
\alpha_{0}\in(0,0.01]
is the small probability that is reserved such that
\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}
does not lie on the extreme tail of the limiting distribution in (
45
).
The main message of the theorem is that there exists an index
m^{*}\in\mathcal{M}
such that its corresponding
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m^{*}]}
is sufficiently close to
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
, where
m^{*}
is defined as the minimizer of
\min_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\|\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}-\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}
.
We combine Theorems
5
and
6
to establish that

\min_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\|{\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}}-\gamma^{*}\|_{2}\leq\min_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\|{\widehat{\gamma}^{[m]}}-\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}+\|\gamma^{*}-\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}\ll n^{-1/2}.

(46)

The above result indicates the success of the proposed perturbation strategy, where there exists a perturbed weight
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m^{*}]}
that converges to
\gamma^{*}
at a rate faster than
n^{-1/2}.

In the following theorem, we establish the coverage validity of the proposed CI in (
40
).

Theorem 7
.

Suppose that
Conditions
(A1)
–
(A5)
hold and
\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})\gg\max\{\sqrt{n\log N/N},\;\sqrt{\log n}\cdot n^{-1/8}\}
. Then the
{\rm CI}
proposed in (
40
) satisfies

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\ \liminf_{M\to\infty}\ \mathbf{P}(\theta_{1}^{*}\in{\rm CI})\geq 1-\alpha,

(47)

where
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
is the pre-specified significance level.

The above theorem suggests that the proposed CI achieves valid coverage uniformly, even when
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
exhibits a nonnormal limiting distribution. The imposed condition
\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})\gg\max\{\sqrt{n\log N/N},\;\sqrt{\log n}\cdot n^{-1/8}\}
requires that
\{\mu^{(l)}\}_{l\in[L]}
are not overly collinear. When this condition holds, we show in (
46
) that there exists one perturbed weight vector
\widehat{\gamma}^{[m^{*}]}
that accurately approximates
\gamma^{*}
with
\|\widehat{\gamma}^{[m^{*}]}-\gamma^{*}\|_{2}\ll n^{-1/2}
.

Next, we analyze the length of the proposed confidence interval in (
40
).

Theorem 8
.

Suppose that
Conditions

(A1)
–
(A5)
hold. Then there exists a positive constant
c_{1}>0
such that the
CI
’s length
{\bf Leng}({\rm CI})
satisfies

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\ \liminf_{M\to\infty}\ \mathbf{P}\left({\bf Leng}({\rm CI})\lesssim\left(t+\frac{z_{\alpha_{0}}}{\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\geq 1-\alpha_{0}-e^{-c_{1}t^{2}},

(48)

where
t
satisfies
c_{0}\leq t\ll\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})n^{1/8}
, with the constant
c_{0}>0
from
Condition
(A1)
,
\alpha_{0}\in(0,0.01]
is the pre-specified parameter used to construct
\mathcal{M}
in (
36
), and
z_{\alpha_{0}}
denotes the upper
\alpha_{0}
quantile of the standard normal distribution.

We now provide several remarks regarding the theorem above. First, the length result in (
48
) establishes a high-probability upper bound on the length of the CI, which is of parametric order
n^{-1/2}
if
\sigma_{L}(\mathcal{U})
is of a constant order. Second, we clarify the role of the value
t
in the upper bound of the length in (
48
). By construction, the proposed CI is formed as a union of
|\mathcal{M}|
individual intervals, each centered at
\widehat{\theta}^{[m]}_{1}
, with a radius on the order of
n^{-1/2}
. The deviation of the resampled centers is
\max_{m\in\mathcal{M}}|\widehat{\theta}^{[m]}_{1}-\theta^{*}_{1}|
, which is governed by
\max_{l}\|\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}-\mu^{(l)}\|_{2}
. The term
t
and the tail bound
\exp(-c_{1}t^{2})
arise from Condition
(A1)
, which assumes
\max_{l}\|\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}-\mu^{(l)}\|_{2}\leq t/\sqrt{n}
. Third, the condition
c_{0}\leq t\ll\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})n^{1/8}
implies that
\sigma_{L}(\mathcal{U})\gg n^{-1/16}
, indicating that
\{\mu^{(l)}\}_{l\in[L]}
cannot be too collinear. In the special case, when
\sigma_{L}(\mathcal{U})
is bounded below by a positive constant, the requirement on
t
is satisfied for any
c_{0}\leq t\ll n^{1/8}
.
Lastly, we note that both the coverage and length properties can be extended directly to the settings for any linear transformation of
\theta^{*}
, enabling inference on general linear contrasts of the parameter vector.

7
Simulations

We start with the description of the simulated data setup. For each source domain
l\in[L]
, we generate
\{X^{(l)}_{i},Y^{(l)}_{i}\}_{i\in[n_{l}]}
in an i.i.d. fashion, where
X^{(l)}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0_{d},{\bf I}_{d})
and
Y^{(l)}_{i}
is sampled from a multinomial distribution over
K+1
classes, with conditional probabilities given by

\mathbb{P}^{(l)}(Y=c|X)=\frac{\exp(\phi^{(l)}_{c}(X))}{\sum_{k=0}^{K}\exp(\phi_{k}^{(l)}(X))},\quad\textrm{for $c=0,1,\dots,K$.}

(49)

Here
\{\phi_{c}^{(l)}(\cdot)\}_{l,c}
could be linear and nonlinear functions and will be specified in the following discussions.
For the target domain, the unlabeled data
\{X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j}\}_{j\in[N]}
are i.i.d. drawn from
\mathbb{Q}_{X}
, which will be specified for each simulation regime. Throughout the following experiments, we set
n_{l}=n
for all
l\in[L]
, and set
N=10,000
, and vary
n,d,L,K,\{\phi^{(l)}_{c}\}_{l,c}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{X}
across different setups. To ensure consistency across simulation rounds, all relevant parameters are generated once and held fixed throughout the 500 independent replications. All reported results below are averaged over these 500 simulations.

Estimation Error.
We empirically evaluate the convergence behavior of the estimator
\widehat{\theta}
, implemented via Algorithm
2
, under two different settings:

(S1)

We consider a binary classification task with
K=1
and
L=2
source domains. We set
\mathbb{Q}_{X}=\mathcal{N}(0.2_{d},{\bf I}_{d})
with
d=20
. For
l\in[L]
, and
k\in\{0,1\}
, we set

\phi^{(l)}_{k}(X)=X^{\intercal}\beta^{(l,k)},\quad\textrm{with}\quad\beta^{(l,0)}=0_{d},\quad\beta^{(l,1)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0_{d},0.25{\bf I}_{d}).

(S2)

We consider a multi-class classification task with
K=3
(i.e., four categories) and
L=4
source domains. We set
\mathbb{Q}_{X}=\mathcal{N}(0.2_{d},0.25{\bf I}_{d})
with dimension
d=5
. For each source
l\in[L]
and category
k\in[K]
, the function
\phi_{k}^{(l)}(\cdot)
in (
49
) is generated as follows:

\phi_{k}^{(l)}(X)=a^{(l,k)}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}w^{(l)}_{j}\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{(X_{j}-\frac{k}{4})^{2}}{4}\right)+b^{(l,k)}X_{1}^{\intercal}X_{2}+c^{(l,k)}\sin\left((X_{3}-X_{4})+\frac{k}{3}\right),

where the parameters
a^{(l,k)},b^{(l,k)},c^{(l,k)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}{\rm Uniform}(-0.5,0.5)
and
w^{(l)}\sim{\rm Dirichlet}({\bf 1}_{d})
.

The difference between the two settings lies in the complexity of the classification task. While
(S1)
is a linear binary classification problem,
(S2)
introduces a highly nonlinear and multi-class classification task. We utilize both settings to highlight the effectiveness of our method under both parametric and nonparametric regimes.
The implementation of Algorithm
2
requires estimating the density ratio
\widehat{\omega}^{(l)}
and the conditional probability
\widehat{f}^{(l)}
for each source
l\in[L]
. For
(S1)
, we primarily employ logistic regression, but we also implement the XGBoost algorithm and observe similar results. For
(S2)
, we apply XGBoost with cross-validation to handle this more complex task.

Figure 7
:
The estimation error
\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}/\sqrt{d}
in settings
(S1)
and
(S2)
. The sample size per source domain
n
, varies in the range of
[200,2000]
, while the unlabeled target data sample size is fixed at
N=10,000
. The results are summarized from 500 simulations, and the orange curves are fitted on the empirical means of 500 estimation errors for each sample size.

In Figure
7
, for each source sample size
n\in\{200,400,...,2000\}
, we report the box-plot of the estimation errors
\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}/\sqrt{d}
over 500 simulations, where the normalization by
\sqrt{d}
ensures the comparative scale across settings. The orange dots on each box correspond to the empirical mean estimation error at the given sample size. We then fit the orange curve to these empirical means across all considered sample sizes to visualize the convergence rate.

In summary, it is evident that in both settings, the estimation error of
\widehat{\theta}
decays at the rate of
n^{-1/2}
, aligning with our theoretical result established in Theorem
4
.

Inference Validation.
In this section, we assess the effectiveness of our inference procedure for constructing the confidence interval for the target parameter
\theta_{1}^{*}
. We introduce two settings involving parametric modeling and provide additional simulations under more complex, highly nonlinear setups in Appendix
E.3
.

(S3)

This setting captures a spectrum of regimes ranging from
regular
to
nonregular
, controlled by the perturbation parameter
\delta
. We consider a binary classification task with
K=1
and
L=2
source domains. We set
\mathbb{Q}_{X}=\mathcal{N}(0.2_{d},{\bf I}_{d})
with
d=20
and
\phi_{k}^{(l)}(X)=X^{\intercal}\beta^{(l,k)}
with

\beta^{(1,0)}=0_{d},\;\beta^{(1,1)}=(0.5+\delta,0.5_{3},0_{d-4})^{\intercal},\;\beta^{(2,0)}=0_{d},\;\beta^{(2,1)}=(0_{4},0.5_{2},0_{d-6})^{\intercal}.

The parameter
\delta\in\{0,0.5,1.0,...,4.0\}
perturbs the first entry of the first source’s class-1 coefficient vector. As
\delta
increases, the discrepancy between the two source grows, increasing the nonregularity of the problem.

(S4)

This setting captures a spectrum of regimes ranging from
regular
to
unstable
, governed by the noise parameter
\sigma
. We consider a multi-class classification task with four categories (
K=3
) and
L=4
source domains. We set
\mathbb{Q}_{X}=\mathcal{N}(0.2_{d},{\bf I}_{d})
with
d=20
and
\phi_{k}^{(l)}(X)=X^{\intercal}\beta^{(l,k)}
with

\beta^{(l,0)}=0_{d},\;\beta^{(l,k)}_{1:5}=(1,1,0,0,0)^{\intercal}+\mathcal{N}(0_{5},\sigma^{2}{\bf I}_{5}),\;\beta^{(l,k)}_{6:d}=0_{d-5}\quad\textrm{for $k=1,2,3$.}

The noise parameter
\sigma\in[0.1,0.5]
controls the level of instability across sources. When
\sigma
is small, the coefficients across sources are nearly identical, inducing strong instability. As
\sigma
increases, the variability between sources grows, alleviating the instability and leading to a more regular setting.

In addition to our proposed approach as detailed in Section
5.2
, we consider the following benchmark inference procedures for comparison in terms of coverage and interval length:

•

Oracle Normality.
This method assumes
(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta^{*}_{1})/\widehat{\rm se}_{\rm norm}\xlongrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, where
\widehat{\rm se}_{\rm norm}
denotes the oracle standard error of
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
calculated from 500 simulations. The corresponding
1-\alpha
confidence interval is constructed as:

(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-z_{\alpha/2}\widehat{\rm se}_{\rm norm},\widehat{\theta}_{1}+z_{\alpha/2}\widehat{\rm se}_{\rm norm})
.

•

Bootstrap.

For the
b
-th bootstrap with
b\in[B]
, we randomly draw observations with replacement from the source and target datasets, and compute the corresponding estimator
\widehat{\theta}^{b}_{1}
via Algorithm
2
. Repeating this process
B=500
times yields a collection of estimators
\{\widehat{\theta}^{b}_{1}\}_{b\in[B]}
, from which we compute the empirical standard error
\widehat{\rm se}_{\rm boot}
. The resulting bootstrap confidence interval is then constructed as
(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-z_{\alpha/2}\widehat{\rm se}_{\rm boot},\widehat{\theta}_{1}+z_{\alpha/2}\widehat{\rm se}_{\rm boot})
.

•

Oracle Bias-awareness (OBA).
It considers that the estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
could exhibit non-negligible bias relative to
\theta^{*}_{1}
. Specifically, it assumes
(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta^{*}_{1})/\widehat{\rm se}_{\rm oba}\xlongrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(b,1)
, where
\widehat{\rm se}_{\rm oba}
denotes the empirical standard error of
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
computed from 500 simulations, and
b
represents the corresponding bias. Notably, we use the oracle knowledge of the bias
|\mathbb{E}\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta^{*}_{1}|
and form the oracle bias-aware confidence interval as

(\widehat{\theta}-\chi,\widehat{\theta}+\chi)
, following the work
(
Armstrong et al. 2020
)
,
where
\chi=\widehat{\rm se}_{\rm oba}\cdot\sqrt{\mathrm{cv}_{\alpha}(|\mathbb{E}\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta^{*}_{1}|^{2}/\widehat{\rm se}_{\rm oba}^{2})}
, and
\mathrm{cv}_{\alpha}\left(B^{2}\right)
denotes the
1-\alpha
quantile of the non-central
\chi^{2}(B^{2})
distribution with one degree of freedom and non-centrality parameter
B^{2}
.

Figure 8
:
Empirical coverage and average interval lengths of different inference methods under two settings:
(S3)
(top panels) with sparse perturbation level
\delta\in\{0,0.5,\ldots,4.0\}
, and
(S4)
(bottom panels) with instability level
\sigma\in\{0.1,0.15,\ldots,0.50\}
. Each point is averaged over 500 simulation rounds.

Figure
8
reports the empirical coverage and average interval lengths for all the aforementioned inference methods. We begin by discussing the results for
(S3)
, shown in the top panels, where we vary the sparse perturbation level
\delta\in[0,0.5,\dots,4.0]
. When
\delta=0
, the setup is regular, and all methods achieve the nominal
95\%
coverage. However, as
\delta
increases, the degree of nonregularity becomes more pronounced. Consequently, both the
Normality
and
Bootstrap
methods fail to maintain valid coverage, with coverage deteriorating as
\delta
grows. In contrast, our
Proposed
method, along with the oracle
OBA
method, retains uniformly valid coverage across all values of
\delta
considered in this experiment. We emphasize that
OBA
relies on oracle knowledge of the true parameter
\theta^{*}_{1}
and is thus not implementable in practice. For interval lengths, our
Proposed
method yields interval lengths that are comparable to those of the oracle
OBA
method.

We now turn to the setup
(S4)
, with results shown in the bottom panels of Figure
8
. In this experiment, we vary the instability level across
\sigma\in\{0.1,0.15,\ldots,0.50\}
. When
\sigma
is small, the coefficients across source domains are highly similar, inducing an unstable estimation regime. As
\sigma
increases, the variation among source coefficients becomes more pronounced, and the instability gradually diminishes. The results show that when
\sigma
is small, both the
Normality
and
OBA
methods fail to achieve valid
95\%
coverage, though their performance improves as
\sigma
increases. In contrast, the
Proposed
method consistently attains the nominal
95\%
coverage across all values of
\sigma
, while achieving comparable lengths to those of the oracle
OBA
method.

In summary, Figure
8
illustrates that the proposed confidence interval in (
40
) achieves uniform coverage even when the empirical CG-DRO model
\widehat{\theta}
exhibits a nonstandard limiting distribution in the
nonregular
and
unstable
settings. Moreover, the confidence interval attains a comparable length to that of the oracle procedure.

Appendix

This appendix provides additional methodological details, proofs of theoretical results, and experimental settings supplementing the main text. We list a summary of the appendix in the following.

•

Section
A
contains the proof of two key approximation results: Theorem
1
and Theorem
5
. We also include the proof of Theorem
2
as it is an integral part of establishing Theorem
5
.

•

Section
B
introduces additional methods and theoretical results omitted from the main text. In particular, we provide more discussions on Mirror Prox algorithm in Section
B.1
, describe the detailed procedures for constructing the DML estimators
\{\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}\}
in Section
B.2
, and establish their theoretical properties in Section
B.3
. Section
B.4
provides the remaining implementation details for the statistical inference procedure.

•

Section
C
contains the proofs of the remaining main theoretical results, while Section
D
presents the proofs of supporting lemmas.

•

Section
E
describes the experimental details within the main article and presents additional simulation studies.

Appendix A
Proof of Approximation Theorems

In this section, we present the proofs of two key approximation results: Theorem
1
and Theorem
5
. These results are of independent interest, as they rigorously quantify the discrepancy between the solutions of the approximate and original minimax optimization problems. Specifically, Theorem
1
establishes convergence on the primal side, while Theorem
5
addresses the dual convergence.
We start with introducing some important notations and lemmas in Section
A.1
and then prove Theorems
1
and
5
in Sections
A.3
and
A.5
, respectively.

The proof of Theorem
1
relies on Proposition
1
and the proof of Theorem
5
relies on the closed-form expressions established in Theorem
2
. Hence, we present the proof of Proposition
1
in Section
A.2
and the proof of Theorem
2
in Section
A.4
.

A.1
Proof Preparations

We begin with the introduction of additional notations and definitions to facilitate the theoretical analysis.
We define the column vector
p(X_{i},\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
as

p(X_{i},\theta)=(p_{1}(X_{i},\theta),\cdots,p_{K}(X_{i},\theta))^{\intercal}\quad\text{with}\quad p_{c}(X_{i},\theta)=\frac{\exp(\theta_{c}^{\intercal}X_{i})}{1+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\exp(\theta_{k}^{\intercal}X_{i})},

(50)

where
p_{c}(X_{i},\theta)
represents the predicted probability for category
c\in[K]
under the parameter
\theta
. We define the matrix

D(X_{i},\theta)={\rm diag}(p(X_{i},\theta))-p(X_{i},\theta)p^{\intercal}(X_{i},\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}.

We use
\nabla S(\theta)
and
H(\theta)
to respectively denote the gradient and Hessian of
S(\theta),
with the following expression

\nabla S(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathbb{Q}_{X}}\left(p(X,\theta)\otimes X\right)\quad\text{and}\quad H(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathbb{Q}_{X}}[D(X,\theta)\otimes XX^{\intercal}].

For their empirical counterparts, given the unlabeled data
\{X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
from the target domain, we also obtain the following gradient and Hessian of
\widehat{S}(\theta)
,

\nabla\widehat{S}(\theta)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}p(X_{i},\theta)\otimes X_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{H}(\theta)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}D(X_{i},\theta)\otimes X_{i}X_{i}^{\intercal}.

We introduce the bounded parameter space centered around
\theta^{*}
,

\Theta_{B}:=\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{1}\leq 1\right\}.

(51)

In the following two lemmas, we establish the positiveness and the continuity of
H(\theta)
. The proofs of the following two lemmas are presented in Sections
D.1
and
D.2
.

Lemma 1
.

Suppose that Condition
(A2)
holds. For all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}
, we have

\lambda_{\min}(H(\theta))>0,

which implies that
S(\theta)
is strictly convex in
\theta
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}
.
Moreover, there exists some constant
c_{1}\in(0,1/2)
such that: for all
\theta\in\Theta_{B}
,

c_{1}^{2}\kappa_{1}\leq\lambda_{\min}(H(\theta))\leq\lambda_{\max}(H(\theta))\leq(1-c_{1})\kappa_{2},

where
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}
are positive constants defined in Condition
(A2)
.

Lemma
1
implies that the function
S(\theta)
is strictly convex over the entire space
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}
, and becomes strongly convex when restricted to the bounded parameter set
\theta\in\Theta_{B}
.

The following lemma establishes the continuity of the Hessian matrix.

Lemma 2
.

Suppose that Condition
(A2)
holds. There exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}
,

\|H(\theta^{\prime})-H(\theta)\|_{2}\leq C\left(\|\theta^{\prime}-\theta\|_{2}+\|\theta^{\prime}-\theta\|_{2}^{2}\right),

(52)

\left\|D(X,\theta^{\prime})-D(X,\theta)\right\|_{2}\leq 6\Delta(X,\theta^{\prime}-\theta)+12\Delta(X,\theta^{\prime}-\theta)^{2},

(53)

with

\Delta(X,\theta^{\prime}-\theta)=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}[X^{\intercal}(\theta_{k}^{\prime}-\theta_{k})]^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Define
n=\min_{1\leq l\leq L}n_{l}.
To facilitate theoretical analysis, we introduce the following events

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}_{0}\cap

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{2}\cap\mathcal{G}_{3},

(54)

\displaystyle\textrm{with}\quad\mathcal{G}_{0}

\displaystyle=\left\{\|\widehat{\mathcal{U}}-\mathcal{U}\|_{2}\leq t\sqrt{d/n}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\{\max_{\theta\in\Theta_{B}}|\widehat{S}(\theta)-S(\theta)|\leq C\sqrt{d\log N/N}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\{\max_{\theta\in\Theta_{B}}\|\nabla\widehat{S}({\theta})-\nabla{S}({\theta})\|_{2}\leq C\sqrt{d\log N/N}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{3}

\displaystyle=\left\{\max_{\theta\in\Theta_{B}}\|\widehat{H}({\theta})-{H}({\theta})\|_{2}\leq C\sqrt{d\log N/N}\right\},

where
t
is any value larger than the positive constant
c_{0}
specified in Condition (A1), and the bounded set
\Theta_{B}
is defined in (
51
).
We now briefly explain these events. Event
\mathcal{G}_{0}
quantifies the distance between the estimated vectors
\{\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}\}_{l}
and their population counterparts
\{\mu^{(l)}\}_{l}
, which holds with high probability under Condition (A1) of the main article. Events
\mathcal{G}_{1}
,
\mathcal{G}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{G}_{3}
provide uniform concentration bounds for the empirical risk function
\widehat{S}(\theta)
, its gradient, and its Hessian, respectively, over the bounded parameter space
\Theta_{B}
. We show that
\mathcal{G}
occurs with high probability in the following lemma, whose proof is presented in
D.3
.

Lemma 3
.

Suppose Conditions
(A1)
and
(A2)
hold. Then there exists a positive constant
c_{1}>0
such that

\mathbf{P}\left(\mathcal{G}_{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{2}\cap\mathcal{G}_{3}\right)\geq 1-N^{-c_{1}d},

and

\mathbf{P}\left(\mathcal{G}\right)\geq 1-N^{-c_{1}d}-e^{-c_{1}t^{2}}-\delta_{n},

(55)

where
t
is any value larger than
c_{0}
, and both
c_{0}
and the vanishing sequence
\delta_{n}\to 0
are specified in Condition
(A1)
.

We use the following lemma to establish the positive definiteness of
\widehat{H}(\theta)
, whose proof is presented in
D.4
.

Lemma 4
.

Suppose that Condition
(A2)
holds, and

N\gtrsim d\log N/\kappa_{1}^{2}.

Then on the event
\mathcal{G}_{3}
, for all
\theta\in\Theta_{B}
,

\lambda_{\rm min}\left(\widehat{H}(\theta)\right)\geq\frac{\kappa_{1}c_{1}^{2}}{2},

where
c_{1}>0
is a constant specified in Lemma
1
and
\kappa_{1}>0
is specified in Condition
(A2)
.

A.2
Proof of Proposition
1

We rewrite the true parameter
\theta^{*}
in (
6
) and the estimator
\widehat{\theta}
in (
8
) as

\theta^{*}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\Phi(\theta),\quad\textrm{with}\quad\Phi(\theta)=\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\left[\theta^{\intercal}\mathcal{U}\gamma+S(\theta)\right].

(56)

\widehat{\theta}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\widehat{\Phi}(\theta),\quad\textrm{with}\quad\widehat{\Phi}(\theta)=\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\left[\theta^{\intercal}\widehat{\mathcal{U}}\gamma+\widehat{S}(\theta)\right].

(57)

The definition of
\Theta_{B}
in (
51
) implies
\theta^{*}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\Theta_{B}}{\Phi}(\theta)
.
To facilitate the discussion, we define
\widehat{\theta}_{B}
as

\widehat{\theta}_{B}\in\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\Theta_{B}}\widehat{\Phi}(\theta),\quad\text{where}\;\Theta_{B}\;\text{is the bounded set defined in}\;\eqref{eq: bound para}.

(58)

It follows from Lemma
4
that, on the event
\mathcal{G}_{3}
, the function
\widehat{\Phi}(\theta)
is strongly convex over
\theta\in\Theta_{B}
and hence
\widehat{\theta}_{B}
is uniquely defined in (
58
).

The main idea of the proof is to show that
\widehat{\theta}_{B}
is the same as
\widehat{\theta}
with a high probability, and then we establish the convergence rate of
\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}
by controlling
\|\widehat{\theta}_{B}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}
.
We start with establishing the convergence rate for
\|\widehat{\theta}_{B}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}.

Since both
\theta^{*}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{B}
belong to
\Theta_{B}
, we apply the strong convexity of
\Phi(\theta)
over the parameter space
\Theta_{B}
, as established in Lemma
4
, and obtain that

\Phi(\widehat{\theta}_{B})-\Phi(\theta^{*})\geq\langle\partial\Phi(\theta^{*}),\widehat{\theta}_{B}-\theta^{*}\rangle+\frac{\kappa_{1}c_{1}^{2}}{2}\|\widehat{\theta}_{B}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\kappa_{1}c_{1}^{2}}{2}\|\widehat{\theta}_{B}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}^{2},

(59)

where
\langle\partial\Phi(\theta^{*}),\widehat{\theta}_{B}-\theta^{*}\rangle\geq 0
since
\theta^{*}
is the minimum value of
\Phi(\cdot)
on the set
\Theta_{B}.

Note that

\displaystyle\Phi(\widehat{\theta}_{B})-\Phi(\theta^{*})

\displaystyle=\Phi(\widehat{\theta}_{B})-\widehat{\Phi}(\widehat{\theta}_{B})+\widehat{\Phi}(\widehat{\theta}_{B})-\widehat{\Phi}(\theta^{*})+\widehat{\Phi}(\theta^{*})-\Phi(\theta^{*})

(60)

\displaystyle\leq\Phi(\widehat{\theta}_{B})-\widehat{\Phi}(\widehat{\theta}_{B})+\widehat{\Phi}(\theta^{*})-{\Phi}(\theta^{*})

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{\theta\in\Theta_{B}}|\widehat{\Phi}(\theta)-\Phi(\theta)|,

where the first inequality holds follows from the definition of
\widehat{\theta}_{B}
being the minimizer of (
58
), implying
\widehat{\Phi}(\widehat{\theta}_{B})-\widehat{\Phi}(\theta^{*})\leq 0
.

By the definitions of
\Phi(\theta)
and
\widehat{\Phi}(\theta)
in (
56
) and (
57
), we have

\left|\widehat{\Phi}(\theta)-\Phi(\theta)\right|\leq\max_{1\leq l\leq L}\left|\theta^{\intercal}\left[\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}-\mu^{(l)}\right]+\widehat{S}(\theta)-{S}(\theta)\right|.

Together with the results in (
59
) and (
60
), we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\theta}_{B}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\max_{\theta\in\Theta_{B}}\max_{1\leq l\leq L}\left|\theta^{\intercal}\left[\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}-\mu^{(l)}\right]+\widehat{S}(\theta)-{S}(\theta)\right|

(61)

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\max_{\theta\in\Theta_{B}}\max_{1\leq l\leq L}\left[\|\theta\|_{2}\|\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}-\mu^{(l)}\|_{2}+|\widehat{S}(\theta)-S(\theta)|\right].

On the event
\mathcal{G}
defined in (
54
), we establish

\max_{\theta\in\Theta_{B}}\max_{1\leq l\leq L}\left[\|\theta\|_{2}\|\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}-\mu^{(l)}\|_{2}+|\widehat{S}(\theta)-S(\theta)|\right]\lesssim t\cdot(1+\|\theta_{*}\|_{2})\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{d\log N}{N}}\lesssim t\sqrt{d/n},

where the last inequality holds since we assume
t\geq c_{0}
in Condition (A1) and
N\geq Cn\log N
and
\|\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq C
in Condition (A2).

Putting the preceding inequality back to (
61
), we establish that, on the event
\mathcal{G},

\|\widehat{\theta}_{B}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq C\sqrt{t}\cdot(d/n)^{1/4},

(62)

for some constant
C>0
. Therefore,

\|\widehat{\theta}_{B}-\theta^{*}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{d}\|\widehat{\theta}_{B}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq C\sqrt{t}(d^{3}/n)^{1/4}\leq 1/2,

(63)

where the last inequality holds if
t\lesssim\sqrt{n/d^{3}}
.

In the following, we prove
\widehat{\theta}\in\Theta_{B}
, which further implies that
\widehat{\theta}_{B}=\widehat{\theta}
. We prove the result by a contradiction argument. We assume that there exists
\widehat{\theta}
outside
\Theta_{B}
that minimizes
\widehat{\Phi}(\theta)
over
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}
, implying
\widehat{\Phi}(\widehat{\theta})\leq\widehat{\Phi}(\widehat{\theta}_{B})
.
Due to the convexity of
\widehat{\Phi}(\theta)
, for any
\nu\in[0,1]
, we have

\widehat{\Phi}(\widehat{\theta}_{B}+t(\widehat{\theta}-\widehat{\theta}_{B}))\leq\nu\widehat{\Phi}(\widehat{\theta})+(1-\nu)\widehat{\Phi}(\widehat{\theta}_{B})\leq\widehat{\Phi}(\widehat{\theta}_{B}).

The pathway
\widehat{\theta}_{B}+\nu(\widehat{\theta}-\widehat{\theta}_{B})
intersects
\Theta_{B}
for some
\nu\in[0,1]
. We denote the intersection point as
\theta_{\rm inter}
, which satisfies
\widehat{\Phi}(\theta_{\rm inter})\leq\widehat{\Phi}(\widehat{\theta}_{B})
and
\theta_{\rm inter}\in\Theta_{B}
with
\|\theta_{\rm inter}-\theta^{*}\|_{1}=1
. Since
\widehat{\theta}_{B}
is the unique minimizer of (
58
),
it implies

\widehat{\theta}_{B}=\theta_{\rm inter}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\widehat{\theta}_{B}-\theta^{*}\|_{1}=1.

The above result contradicts (
63
) and hence
\widehat{\theta}\in\Theta_{B}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{B}=\widehat{\theta}
.
Then results in (
62
) and (
63
) imply the following results,

\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq C\sqrt{t}\cdot(d/n)^{1/4}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{1}\leq 1/2.

(64)

Together with Lemma
3
which controls the probability of the event
\mathcal{G}
, we establish that (
17
) holds with probability at least
1-N^{-c_{1}d}-e^{-c_{1}t^{2}}-\delta_{n}
.

A.3
Proof of Theorem
1

To facilitate our discussion, we denote

g(\theta)=\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\theta^{\intercal}\mathcal{U}\gamma
with
\mathcal{U}=(\mu^{(1)},\mu^{(2)},...,\mu^{(L)})\in\mathbb{R}^{dK\times L}
as defined in (
26
), then
\theta^{*}
and
\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}
, defined in (
6
) and (
20
), are expressed as,

\theta^{*}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}g(\theta)+S(\theta),\quad\textrm{and}\quad\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}g(\theta)+Q(\theta).

Proof of (
22
).

For the global minimizer
\theta^{*}
, its optimality condition implies

0\in\partial g(\theta^{*})+\nabla S(\theta^{*}),

where
\partial g(\theta)
denotes the subgradient of the convex function
g(\theta)
.
Meanwhile, the optimality condition of
{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}
implies

0\in\partial g({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap})+\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})+H(\widehat{\theta})({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-\widehat{\theta}).

That is, there exist
s_{1}\in\partial g(\theta^{*}_{\rm ap})
and
s_{2}\in\partial g(\theta^{*})
such that

s_{1}+\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})+H(\widehat{\theta})({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-\widehat{\theta})=0\quad\text{and}\quad s_{2}+\nabla S(\theta^{*})=0.

(65)

Since
g(\cdot)
is convex with a monotone subdifferential operator,
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
satisfy

\langle s_{1}-s_{2},{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-\theta^{*}\rangle\geq 0.
We apply the expressions in (
65
) and obtain

\langle\nabla S(\theta^{*})-\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})-H(\widehat{\theta})({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-\widehat{\theta}),{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-\theta^{*}\rangle\geq 0.

Rearranging the terms, we obtain that

\left\langle\nabla S(\theta^{*})-\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})-H(\widehat{\theta})({\theta}^{*}-\widehat{\theta}),\;{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-\theta^{*}\right\rangle\geq({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-{\theta}^{*})^{\intercal}H(\widehat{\theta})({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-{\theta}^{*}).

Therefore, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

\displaystyle({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-{\theta}^{*})^{\intercal}H(\widehat{\theta})({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-{\theta}^{*})\leq\left\|\nabla S(\theta^{*})-\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})-H(\widehat{\theta})({\theta}^{*}-\widehat{\theta})\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}.

(66)

Note that

\nabla S(\theta^{*})-\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})-H(\widehat{\theta})({\theta}^{*}-\widehat{\theta})=\int_{\nu=0}^{1}\left[H\left(\widehat{\theta}+\nu(\theta^{*}-\widehat{\theta})\right)-H(\widehat{\theta})\right]({\theta}^{*}-\widehat{\theta})d\nu.

The above expression implies

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla S(\theta^{*})-\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})-H(\widehat{\theta})({\theta}^{*}-\widehat{\theta})\right\|_{2}\leq\int_{\nu=0}^{1}\left\|H\left(\widehat{\theta}+\nu(\theta^{*}-\widehat{\theta})\right)-H(\widehat{\theta})\right\|_{2}\left\|{\theta}^{*}-\widehat{\theta}\right\|_{2}d\nu.

(67)

Note that we have established in (
52
) the Lipschitz continuity of the Hessian function, that is,

\left\|H\left(\widehat{\theta}+\nu(\theta^{*}-\widehat{\theta})\right)-H(\widehat{\theta})\right\|_{2}\leq C\left(\nu\|\theta^{*}-\widehat{\theta}\|_{2}+\nu^{2}\|\theta^{*}-\widehat{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

(68)

In the proof of Proposition
1
, we have established in (
64
) that, on the event
\mathcal{G}
,

\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq 1/2\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widehat{\theta}\in\Theta_{B}.

Together with (
68
), we establish

\left\|\nabla S(\theta^{*})-\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})-H(\widehat{\theta})({\theta}^{*}-\widehat{\theta})\right\|_{2}\leq C\cdot\|\theta^{*}-\widehat{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}.

Putting the above inequality back to (
66
), we have

\lambda_{\min}(H(\widehat{\theta}))\cdot\|{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C\|{\theta}^{*}-\widehat{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\|{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}.

(69)

Since
\widehat{\theta}\in\Theta_{B}
on the event
\mathcal{G}
, we apply Lemma
1
and establish that
\lambda_{\rm min}(H(\widehat{\theta}))\geq\kappa_{1}c_{1}^{2}
for some positive constant
c_{1}>0
. Consequently, on the event
\mathcal{G}
, (
69
) implies

\|{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq C\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim t\sqrt{d/n},

where the last inequality holds due to (
64
) established in the proof of Proposition
1
. Lastly, we leverage Lemma
3
to control the probability of the event
\mathcal{G}
.

Proof of (
23
).

To facilitate the theoretical analysis, we define
\widehat{g}(\theta)=\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\theta^{\intercal}\widehat{\mathcal{U}}\gamma
, then
\widehat{\theta}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}
, defined in (
8
) and (
21
), can be equivalently expressed as:

\widehat{\theta}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\widehat{g}(\theta)+\widehat{S}(\theta)\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\widehat{g}(\theta)+\widehat{Q}(\theta).

By the optimality conditions for
\widehat{\theta}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}
, we obtain that there exist
s_{1}\in\partial\widehat{g}(\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap})
and
s_{2}\in\partial\widehat{g}(\widehat{\theta})
such that

s_{1}+\nabla\widehat{S}(\widehat{\theta})+\widehat{H}(\widehat{\theta})(\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}-\widehat{\theta})=0,\quad\text{and}\quad s_{2}+\nabla\widehat{S}(\widehat{\theta})=0.

(70)

Since the function
\widehat{g}(\cdot)
is convex towards
\theta
, the subdifferential operator is monotone in the sense that

\langle s_{1}-s_{2},\;\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}-\widehat{\theta}\rangle\geq 0.

Together with the corresponding expressions of
s_{1},s_{2}
in (
70
), we establish that:

0\leq\langle\widehat{H}(\widehat{\theta})(\widehat{\theta}-\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}),\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}-\widehat{\theta}\rangle=-(\widehat{\theta}-\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap})^{\intercal}\widehat{H}(\widehat{\theta})(\widehat{\theta}-\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}).

It follows from Lemma
4
that, on the event
\mathcal{G}
,
\widehat{H}(\widehat{\theta})
is positive definite and hence
\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}=\widehat{\theta}
. Together with Lemma
3
, we establish that
\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}=\widehat{\theta}
, with probability at least
1-N^{-c_{1}d}-e^{-c_{1}t^{2}}-\delta_{n}
.

A.4
Proof of Theorem
2

In the following, we mainly focus on the proof of (
27
) and the proof of (
28
) can be established with a similar argument. To establish the closed form, it suffices to show that
\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}
, defined in (
27
), uniquely minimizes
\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
,
where

\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)=\theta^{\intercal}\mathcal{U}\gamma+Q(\theta),\quad\textrm{with}\quad Q(\theta)=\langle\nabla S(\widehat{\theta}),\theta-\widehat{\theta}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\widehat{\theta})^{\intercal}H(\widehat{\theta})(\theta-\widehat{\theta}).

(71)

To complete the proof, we start with justifying the following lemma and shall point out that Lemma
1
establishes that
H(\widehat{\theta})
is positive definite.

Lemma 5
.

Suppose Condition
(A3)
holds, and
H(\widehat{\theta})
is positive definite. Then
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
in (
24
) and
\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}
in (
25
) are uniquely defined.

Since
H(\widehat{\theta})
is positive definite, the function
\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
is strictly convex towards
\theta
for any fixed
\gamma\in\Delta^{L}
.
By taking
\nabla_{\theta}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)=0
, we obtain the following expression of the minimizer

\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)=\widehat{\theta}-[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\left(\mathcal{U}\gamma+\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})\right).

(72)

We further express the optimal value
F(\gamma)
defined in (
24
) as follows:

F(\gamma):=\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{U}\gamma+\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})\right)^{\intercal}[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\left(\mathcal{U}\gamma+\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})\right)+\widehat{\theta}^{\intercal}\mathcal{U}\gamma.

(73)

Since
H(\widehat{\theta})
is positive definite, we apply Condition (A3) to establish
F(\gamma)
is strictly concave towards
\gamma
, which justifies the uniqueness of the minimizer
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}=\mathop{\arg\max}_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}F(\gamma)
. Moreover, by plugging the weight vector
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
into (
72
),
\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}
is uniquely defined as

\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}:=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})=\widehat{\theta}-[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\left(\mathcal{U}{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap}+\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})\right).

(74)

We introduce the following lemma establishing that
({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap},{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})
is a saddle point of
\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
, whose proof is deferred to Section
D.5
.

Lemma 6
.

Suppose Conditions
(A3)
holds, and
H(\widehat{\theta})
is positive definite. Then
({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap},{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})
is a saddle point for
\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
over
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}
and
\gamma\in\Delta^{L}
:

\phi_{\rm ap}({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap},\gamma)\leq\phi_{\rm ap}({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap},{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})\leq\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap}),\quad\textrm{for all $\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}$ and $\gamma\in\Delta^{L}$}.

(75)

The saddle point

({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap},{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})
implies that

\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)\leq\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\phi_{\rm ap}({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap},\gamma)=\phi_{\rm ap}({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap},{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})=\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})\leq\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma),

(76)

where the middle two equalities hold due to (
75
).
Together with the fact that

\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)\geq\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma),

we obtain that the equalities hold throughout in (
76
), with

\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\phi_{\rm ap}({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap},\gamma)=\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma).

That is,

{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}\in\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma).

Since
\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
is strictly convex towards
\theta
for each fixed
\gamma
, we have
\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
is strictly convex towards
\theta
. Therefore,
{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}
is the unique minimizer of
\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
such that

{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma).

Proof of (
28
).

To establish the closed form of the problem in (
21
), it suffices to show that
\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}
, defined in (
28
), uniquely minimizes
\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm ap}(\theta)=\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
, where

\widehat{\phi}_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)=\theta^{\intercal}\widehat{\mathcal{U}}\gamma+Q(\theta),\quad\textrm{with}\quad\widehat{Q}(\theta)=\langle\nabla\widehat{S}(\widehat{\theta}),\theta-\widehat{\theta}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\widehat{\theta})^{\intercal}\widehat{H}(\widehat{\theta})(\theta-\widehat{\theta}).

We shall mainly highlight the difference in the following. Since
\widehat{\theta}\in\Theta_{B}
on the event
\mathcal{G}
, Lemma
4
ensures that
\lambda_{\rm min}(\widehat{H}(\widehat{\theta}))\geq c^{\prime}
for some constant
c^{\prime}>0
. This implies that
\widehat{\phi}_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
is strongly convex towards
\theta
for any fixed
\gamma\in\Delta^{L}
, and thus the function
\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
is also strongly convex in
\theta
.

Similarly to Lemma
6
, we can show that on the event
\mathcal{G}
,
(\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap},\widehat{\gamma}_{\rm ap})
forms a saddle point for
\widehat{\phi}_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
over
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}
and
\gamma\in\Delta^{L}
. We omit the proof as it essentially follows the similar proof of Lemma
6
. Then we have
\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}\in\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
. Together with the strong convexity of
\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
, we conclude that
\widehat{\theta}_{\rm ap}
is the unique minimizer of
\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)
on the event
\mathcal{G}
. Lastly, we apply Lemma
3
which controls the probability of the event
\mathcal{G}
to complete the proof.

A.5
Proof of Theorem
5

We recall the definition of
\Theta_{\rm loc}
in (
42
)

\Theta_{\rm loc}=\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq 2\tau\sqrt{d/n}\right\}\quad\text{with}\quad\tau:=C\left(1+\frac{1}{\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})}\right)t,

for some constant
C>0
.
Note that for
\theta\in\Theta_{\rm loc}
, on the event
\mathcal{G}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|S(\theta)-Q(\theta)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|S(\theta)-\left[S(\widehat{\theta})+\langle\nabla S(\widehat{\theta}),\theta-\widehat{\theta}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\widehat{\theta})^{\intercal}H(\widehat{\theta})(\theta-\widehat{\theta})\right]\right|

(77)

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{2}\int_{\nu=0}^{1}(\theta-\widehat{\theta})^{\intercal}\left[H(\widehat{\theta}+\nu(\theta-\widehat{\theta}))-H(\widehat{\theta})\right](\theta-\widehat{\theta})d\nu\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}\|_{2}^{3}\leq C\left(\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}+\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\right)^{3}\leq C\tau^{3}(d/n)^{3/2},

where the first inequality follows from the continuity property of
H(\cdot)
established in (
52
) of Lemma
2
, and the last inequality follows from the definition of
\Theta_{\rm loc}
and Theorem
4
.

Recall that

\phi(\theta,\gamma)=\theta^{\intercal}\mathcal{U}\gamma+S(\theta),\quad\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)=\theta^{\intercal}\mathcal{U}\gamma+Q(\theta).

We introduce or recall the following notations to facilitate discussions,

F^{*}(\gamma)=\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\phi(\theta,\gamma),\quad\textrm{and}\quad F(\gamma)=\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma)

(78)

and

F^{*}_{\rm loc}(\gamma)=\min_{\theta\in\Theta_{\rm loc}}\phi(\theta,\gamma),\quad\textrm{and}\quad F_{\rm loc}(\gamma)=\min_{\theta\in\Theta_{\rm loc}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma).

(79)

We recall the definitions of
\gamma^{*}
in (
41
) and
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
in (
24
) as

\gamma^{*}\in\mathop{\arg\max}_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}F^{*}_{\rm loc}(\gamma)\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}F(\gamma).

(80)

The following proof consists of two steps:

Step 1.

We first establish that, on the event
\mathcal{G}
,

F_{\rm loc}(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})-F_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*})\lesssim\tau^{3}(d/n)^{3/2}.

(81)

Step 2.

We then show that, on the event
\mathcal{G}
,

F_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap})=F(\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap})\quad\text{and}\quad F_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*})=F(\gamma^{*}).

Together with the first step, we further leverage the strong concavity of the function
F(\gamma)
for
\gamma\in\Delta^{L}
, as established in (
73
), to obtain the convergence rate of
\|\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}-\gamma^{*}\|_{2}
.

A.5.1
Proof for Step 1

We now establish an upper bound for
F_{\rm loc}(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})-F_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*}).

For any
\gamma\in\Delta^{L}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|F_{\rm loc}(\gamma)-F^{*}_{\rm loc}(\gamma)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\min_{\theta\in\Theta_{\rm loc}}\left\{\theta^{\intercal}\mathcal{U}\gamma+Q(\theta)\right\}-\min_{\theta\in\Theta_{\rm loc}}\left\{\theta^{\intercal}\mathcal{U}\gamma+S(\theta)\right\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\theta\in\Theta_{\rm loc}}\left|\theta^{\intercal}\mathcal{U}\gamma+S(\theta)-\theta^{\intercal}\mathcal{U}\gamma-Q(\theta)\right|

\displaystyle=\max_{\theta\in\Theta_{\rm loc}}|S(\theta)-Q(\theta)|\leq C\tau^{3}(d/n)^{3/2},

where the last inequality follows from (
77
). Moreover, the following holds:

\displaystyle F_{\rm loc}(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})-F_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*})

\displaystyle=F_{\rm loc}(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})-F_{\rm loc}^{*}(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})+F_{\rm loc}^{*}(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})-F_{\rm loc}^{*}(\gamma^{*})+F^{*}_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*})-F_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*})

\displaystyle\leq F_{\rm loc}(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})-F_{\rm loc}^{*}(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})+F_{\rm loc}^{*}(\gamma^{*})-F_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*})

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\left|F_{\rm loc}(\gamma)-F^{*}_{\rm loc}(\gamma)\right|,

where the first inequality holds since the definition of
\gamma^{*}
in (
41
) implies
F^{*}_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap})\leq F^{*}_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*})
.
Combining the above two inequalities, we establish (
81
).

A.5.2
Proof for Step 2

In the following, we shall show that on the left-hand side of the above inequality,
F_{\rm loc}(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})=F(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})
and
F_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*})=F(\gamma^{*})
. Then together with the strongly concavity of
F(\gamma)
for
\gamma\in\Delta^{L}
, as established in (
73
), we obtain the convergence rate of
\|\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*}-\gamma^{*}\|_{2}
.

We begin with the proof of
F_{\rm loc}(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})=F(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})
.
On the event
\mathcal{G}
, Lemma
6
establishes that
\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta^{*}_{\rm ap},\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap})\leq\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap})
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}
. Therefore, for a smaller parameter set
\Theta_{\rm loc}
, the inequality still holds such that:

\phi_{\rm ap}({\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap},{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})\leq\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})\quad\textrm{for all $\theta\in\Theta_{\rm loc}$}.

In addition, by Theorem
1
, we have shown that, on the event
\mathcal{G}
,

\|{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq Ct\sqrt{d/{n}}\leq\tau\sqrt{d/n},

(82)

where the last inequality holds since
\tau:=Ct(1+\frac{1}{\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})})\geq Ct.

Hence,
{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}\in\Theta_{\rm loc}
. The preceding two inequalities imply that
{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}\in\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\Theta_{\rm loc}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})
, with

\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta_{\rm ap}^{*},{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})=\min_{\theta\in\Theta_{\rm loc}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})=F_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}),

(83)

where the second equality follows from the definition of
F_{\rm loc}(\gamma)
in (
79
).
Note that the definition
\theta^{*}_{\rm ap}
in (
25
) implies

\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta_{\rm ap}^{*},{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})=\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})=F(\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}),

where the last equality holds from the definition of
F(\gamma)
in (
78
).
Therefore, we establish that

\displaystyle F_{\rm loc}(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})=F(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*}).

(84)

Next, we shall establish that
F_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*})=F(\gamma^{*})
, which, together with (
84
), implies an upper bound for
\|\gamma^{*}-\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}
. We divide the discussion into two cases for
\gamma^{*}
, based on whether the following condition holds or not:

\left\|[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\mathcal{U}(\gamma^{*}-{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})\right\|_{2}\leq\tau\sqrt{d/n}.

(85)

Case 1:
\gamma^{*}
satisfies Condition (
85
).

We define

\theta(\gamma^{*}):=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma^{*}),

(86)

and apply (
72
) to obtain

\theta(\gamma^{*})=\widehat{\theta}-[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\left(\mathcal{U}\gamma^{*}+\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})\right).

In (
27
), we show that
{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}
admits the form
{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}=\widehat{\theta}-[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\left(\mathcal{U}\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}+\nabla S(\widehat{\theta})\right)
. Therefore, we establish that

\|\theta(\gamma^{*})-{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}=\left\|[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\mathcal{U}(\gamma^{*}-{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})\right\|_{2}\leq\tau\sqrt{d/n},

where the inequality holds due to Condition (
85
).
We now apply the above inequality together with (
82
) and establish

\|\theta(\gamma^{*})-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq\|\theta(\gamma^{*})-{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}+\|{\theta}^{*}_{\rm ap}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq 2\tau\sqrt{d/n}.

Therefore, we conclude that
\theta(\gamma^{*})\in\Theta_{\rm loc}
. Since
\theta(\gamma^{*})
is the global minimizer of
\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma^{*})
over the entire space
\mathbb{R}^{dK}
as defined in (
86
), we obtain that
\theta(\gamma^{*})
is also the minimizer over the local parameter set
\Theta_{\rm loc}
. Formally, this yields

\phi_{\rm ap}\left(\theta(\gamma^{*}),\gamma^{*}\right)=\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{dK}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma^{*})=\min_{\theta\in\Theta_{\rm loc}}\phi_{\rm ap}(\theta,\gamma^{*}).

Applying the expressions of
F(\gamma)
and
F_{\rm loc}(\gamma)
in (
78
) and (
79
), respectively, we establish that on the event
\mathcal{G}
, when (
85
) holds,

F_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*})=F(\gamma^{*}).

(87)

Putting (
84
) and (
87
) back to (
81
), we establish that

\displaystyle F(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})-F(\gamma^{*})\lesssim\tau^{3}(d/n)^{3/2}.

(88)

The optimality condition of
\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}
, which maximizes
F(\gamma)
for
\gamma\in\Delta^{L}
as defined in (
24
), implies that

\langle\nabla F(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*}),\gamma-\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*}\rangle\leq 0,\quad\textrm{for all $\gamma\in\Delta^{L}$.}

We substitute
\gamma
with
\gamma^{*}
in the above inequality to obtain that

\langle\nabla F(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*}),\gamma^{*}-\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*}\rangle\leq 0.

(89)

Moreover, on the event
\mathcal{G}
,
it follows from (
73
) that
F(\gamma)
is strongly concave for
\gamma\in\Delta^{L}
,
with its Hessian being expressed as

\nabla^{2}F(\gamma)=-\mathcal{U}^{\intercal}[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\mathcal{U}\preceq-\lambda_{\rm min}([H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1})\sigma^{2}_{L}(\mathcal{U})\cdot{\bf I}_{L}\preceq-\frac{\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})}{\kappa_{2}(1-c_{1})}\cdot{\bf I}_{L},

where
\sigma_{L}(\mathcal{U})>0
is the
L
-th largest singular value of the matrix
\mathcal{U}
as assumed in Condition (A3), and the last inequality follows from Lemma
1
with a constant
c_{1}\in(0,1/2)
.
The strong concavity of
F(\cdot)
implies

\displaystyle F(\gamma^{*})

\displaystyle\leq F(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})+\langle\nabla F(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*}),\gamma^{*}-\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*}\rangle-\frac{\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})}{2\kappa_{2}(1-c_{1})}\|\gamma^{*}-{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq F(\gamma_{\rm ap}^{*})-\frac{\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})}{2\kappa_{2}(1-c_{1})}\|\gamma^{*}-{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}^{2},

where the second inequality holds due to (
89
).

Combining the above inequality with (
88
), we further establish that on the event
\mathcal{G}
,

\|\gamma^{*}-{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\sigma_{L}(\mathcal{U})}\tau^{3/2}(d/n)^{3/4}.

Since
\tau^{1/2}=\sqrt{Ct(1+\frac{1}{\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})})}\gtrsim\frac{1}{\sigma_{L}(\mathcal{U})}
, where
t
is any value larger than
c_{0}
as specified in Condition (A1), we further establish that

\|\gamma^{*}-{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}\lesssim\tau^{2}(d/n)^{3/4}.

(90)

Case 2:
\gamma^{*}
does not satisfy Condition (
85
).
In this case,
\gamma^{*}
satisfies

\left\|[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\mathcal{U}(\gamma^{*}-{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})\right\|_{2}>\tau\sqrt{d/n}.

We then define
\gamma_{\rm proj}
as follows:

\gamma_{\rm proj}={\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap}+\nu(\gamma^{*}-{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap}),\quad\textrm{with}\quad\nu=\frac{\tau\sqrt{d/n}}{\left\|[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\mathcal{U}(\gamma^{*}-{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})\right\|_{2}}\in(0,1).

By the above definition, we have
\left\|[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\mathcal{U}(\gamma_{\rm proj}-{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})\right\|_{2}=\tau\sqrt{d/n}
, thus

\tau^{2}\frac{d}{n}=\|[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\mathcal{U}(\gamma_{\rm proj}-{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})\|_{2}^{2}\leq\lambda_{\rm max}(\mathcal{U}^{\intercal}[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-2}\mathcal{U})\|\gamma_{\rm proj}-{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}^{2}.

Therefore, we obtain that

\|\gamma_{\rm proj}-{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}\geq\frac{\tau\sqrt{d/n}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\rm max}(\mathcal{U}^{\intercal}[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-2}\mathcal{U})}}\gtrsim\tau\sqrt{d/n},

(91)

where the second inequality holds from Lemma
1
that
\lambda_{\rm min}(H(\widehat{\theta}))\geq c^{\prime}
on the event
\mathcal{G}
and
\|\mathcal{U}\|_{2}\leq C
assumed in Condition (A4).

Recall that
F_{\rm loc}(\gamma)
is concave towards
\gamma\in\Delta^{L}
, as defined in (
79
). Therefore, we have

\displaystyle F_{\rm loc}(\gamma_{\rm proj})=F_{\rm loc}(\nu\gamma^{*}+(1-\nu){\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})\geq\nu\cdot F_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*})+(1-\nu)\cdot F_{\rm loc}({\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap}).

Then it holds that

F_{\rm loc}({\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})-F_{\rm loc}(\gamma_{\rm proj})\leq\nu\cdot\left(F_{\rm loc}({\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})-F_{\rm loc}(\gamma^{*})\right)\leq C\tau^{3}(d/n)^{3/2},

(92)

where the last inequality follows from (
81
) and
\nu\in(0,1)
.

Since
\gamma_{\rm proj}
satisfies
\left\|[H(\widehat{\theta})]^{-1}\mathcal{U}(\gamma_{\rm proj}-{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap})\right\|_{2}\leq\tau\sqrt{d/n}
, we shall apply the same argument as those from (
86
) to (
87
) in Case 1, by replacing
\gamma^{*}
by
\gamma_{\rm proj}
, and establish

F_{\rm loc}(\gamma_{\rm proj})=F(\gamma_{\rm proj}).

Putting the above equality and (
84
) back to (
92
), we establish that

F(\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap})-F(\gamma_{\rm proj})\leq C\tau^{3}(d/n)^{3/2}.

Lastly, we essentially follow the argument as those from (
88
) to (
90
) in Case 1, by replacing
\gamma^{*}
by
\gamma_{\rm proj}
, and establish that

\|\gamma_{\rm proj}-{\gamma}^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}\leq C\tau^{2}(d/n)^{3/4}.

(93)

As we consider the value
t
that satisfies
t\ll\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})(n/d)^{1/4}
, it holds that

\tau\asymp(1+\frac{1}{\sigma_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U})})t\ll(d/n)^{-1/4}.

Then, we have

C\tau^{2}(d/n)^{3/4}\ll\tau(d/n)^{1/2}.

Therefore, the inequality in (
93
) contradicts (
91
) on the event
\mathcal{G}
. Then, Case 2 does not happen. To conclude, we have shown that only Case 1 happens, for which we establish the convergence rate with
\|\gamma^{*}-\gamma^{*}_{\rm ap}\|_{2}\lesssim\tau^{2}(d/n)^{3/4}
, as in (
90
) on the event
\mathcal{G}
. Lastly, we leverage Lemma
3
to control the probability of the event
\mathcal{G}
.

Appendix B
Additional Method and Theory

B.1
Additional Discussions for Algorithm
1

For completeness, we give the expressions of the gradient of
\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma)
in (
8
) with respect to
\theta
and
\gamma
:

\nabla_{\theta}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma)=\left(\left[\sum_{l=1}^{L}\gamma_{l}\cdot\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{(l)}+\nabla_{\theta_{1}}\widehat{S}(\theta)\right]^{\intercal},\cdots,\left[\sum_{l=1}^{L}\gamma_{l}\cdot\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}_{K}+\nabla_{\theta_{K}}\widehat{S}(\theta)\right]^{\intercal}\right)^{\intercal},

\nabla_{\gamma}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma)=\left(\theta^{\intercal}\widehat{\mu}^{(1)},\cdots,\theta^{\intercal}\widehat{\mu}^{(L)}\right).

The convergence result of the Mirror Prox algorithm in smooth convex-concave settings, as in our case, has been established in the literature; see, for example,
Mokhtari et al. 2020a
and Section 4 of
Bubeck 2015
. In particular, with
\widehat{\theta}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bar{\theta}_{t}
and
\widehat{\gamma}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bar{\gamma}_{t}
, the duality gap on the averaged iterate
(\widehat{\theta},\widehat{\gamma})
satisfies

\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}(\widehat{\theta},\gamma)-\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{Kd}}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\widehat{\gamma})\lesssim T^{-1}.

Furthermore, we observe that

\displaystyle\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}(\widehat{\theta},\gamma)-\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{Kd}}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\widehat{\gamma})

\displaystyle\geq\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}(\widehat{\theta},\gamma)-\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{Kd}}\left[\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma)\right]+\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\left[\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{Kd}}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma)\right]-\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{Kd}}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\widehat{\gamma})

\displaystyle\geq\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}(\widehat{\theta},\gamma)-\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{Kd}}\left[\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma)\right],

where the first inequality holds as min-max larger than max-min.
The combination of the above two inequalities yields

0\leq\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}(\widehat{\theta},\gamma)-\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{Kd}}\left[\max_{\gamma\in\Delta^{L}}\widehat{\phi}(\theta,\gamma)\right]\lesssim T^{-1}.

Hence, Algorithm
1
globally solves the minimax optimization problem in (
8
) with error decaying at the rate
\mathcal{O}(T^{-1})
.
In contrast, without computing intermediate points for gradient evaluation as in the Mirror Prox, the single-step algorithm with gradient descent-mirror ascent yields a slower optimization convergence rate of
\mathcal{O}(T^{-1/2})
, as discussed in Section 5.2 of
Bubeck 2015
.

B.2
Construction of DML Estimators
\{\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}\}
Under Covariate Shift

In this subsection, we provide the detailed construction of the DML estimators
\{\widehat{\mu}^{(l)}\}_{l\in[L]}
, which were omitted from Section
3.2
in the main text. Specifically, we detail the implementation of the components
\{\widehat{f}^{(l)},\widehat{\omega}^{(l)}\}_{l\in[L]}
appearing in equation (
14
).

To correct the bias of the plug-in estimator, it is crucial that the fitted models be independent of the data used for bias correction; see the discussions in
Step 2
of Section
3.2
. To ensure such independence, we employ a cross-fitting strategy. Specifically, for each source
l\in[L]
, we randomly partition the samples
\{X^{(l)}_{i},Y^{(l)}_{i}\}_{i\in[n_{l}]}
into two disjoint subsets
\mathcal{A}_{l}
and
\mathcal{B}_{l}
. The models fitted on one subset are thereby independent of the data in the other subset. Without loss of generality, we write
\mathcal{A}_{l}=\{1,2,...,\lfloor n_{l}/2\rfloor\}
and
\mathcal{B}_{l}=[n_{l}]\setminus\mathcal{A}_{l}
.
For each source
l\in[L]
, we fit the conditional probability models on each subset separately, yielding estimators
\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{B}}
, using data in
\mathcal{A}_{l}
and
\mathcal{B}_{l}
, respectively.

Now, we describe the construction of
\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{A}}
, and the same procedure applies to
\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{B}}
.
Given the data
\{X^{(l)}_{i},Y^{(l)}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{A}_{l}}
, where
Y^{(l)}_{i}\in\{0,1,...,K\}
,
\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\Delta^{K}
is learned by solving the following problem:

\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{A}}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}_{l}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}_{l}}\ell(f(X^{(l)}_{i}),Y^{(l)}_{i})+\lambda\cdot\Omega(f),

where
\ell(f(x),y)
is the cross-entropy loss with the predicted probability
f(x)
,
\Omega(f)
is a regularization penalty, and
\lambda\geq 0
is the parameter tuned by cross-validation. Here
\mathcal{F}
is the function class associated with machine learning methods, say XGBoost, random forest classifiers, and neural networks.

Using the fitted models
\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{A}}(\cdot)
and
\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot)
, we define the following prediction for both source and target data,

\widehat{f}^{(l)}(X^{(l)}_{i})=\begin{cases}\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{(l)}_{i})&\textrm{if $i\in\mathcal{B}_{l}$}\\
\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{B}}(X^{(l)}_{i})&\textrm{if $i\in\mathcal{A}_{l}$}\end{cases},\;\textrm{and}\quad\widehat{f}^{(l)}(X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j})=\frac{1}{2}\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j})+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{f}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{B}}(X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j}).

(94)

This construction ensures that the function
\widehat{f}^{(l)}(\cdot)
is independent of the sample to which the function is applied, and such a property is essential for conducting the bias correction for generic machine learning algorithms.

We apply a similar sample-splitting procedure to construct the density ratio estimators
\{\omega^{(l)}\}_{l\in[L]}
, which also require independence from the data used for bias correction. Specifically, for each source
l\in[L]
, we estimate the density ratio
\widehat{\omega}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{A}}
using the source covariates
\{X^{(l)}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{A}_{l}}
together with the target covariates
\{X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j}\}_{j\in[N]}
, and we estimate
\widehat{\omega}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{B}}
using the source covariates
\{X^{(l)}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{l}}
and the same target covariates. Various approaches for estimating density ratios have been proposed in the literature
(
Sugiyama et al. 2007
;
Gretton et al. 2009
;
Nguyen et al. 2010
;
Sugiyama et al. 2012
)
, and we describe a concrete method based on Bayes’ formula in the following.

We detail the construction of the density ratio estimator
\widehat{\omega}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{A}}
by Bayes’ formula; the similar procedure applies to
\widehat{\omega}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{B}}
. To begin, we merge the source covariates
\{X^{(l)}_{i}\}_{\mathcal{A}_{l}}
with the target covariates
\{X^{\mathbb{Q}}_{j}\}_{j\in[N]}
, and denote the combined dataset as
\{\widetilde{X}^{(l)}_{i}\}_{i\in[|\mathcal{A}_{l}|+N]}
, where the first
|\mathcal{A}_{l}|
samples are from the
l
-th source domain (subset
\mathcal{A}_{l}
), and the remaining
N
samples are from the target domain. We introduce an indicator random variable
G^{(l)}\in\{0,1\}
to denote the domain label of
\widetilde{X}^{(l)}
, such that:

G^{(l)}=\begin{cases}1&\textrm{if $\widetilde{X}^{(l)}$ is drawn from the $l$-th source domain}\\
0&\textrm{if $\widetilde{X}^{(l)}$ is drawn from the target domain}\end{cases}.

Let
p(x|G^{(l)}=g)
denote the conditional density of
\widetilde{X}^{(l)}
given the domain label
G^{(l)}=g
, for
g\in\{0,1\}
. Then we define the source and target covariate densities as:

p_{X}^{(l)}(x):=p(x|G_{i}^{(l)}=0),\quad\textrm{and}\quad q_{X}(x)=p(x|G^{(l)}=1).

The covariate density ratio between the target and the
l
-th source domain is given by

\omega^{(l)}(x):=\frac{q_{X}(x)}{p_{X}^{(l)}(x)}=\frac{p(x|G^{(l)}=1)}{p(x|G_{i}^{(l)}=0)}.

Let
p(G^{(l)}=g)
denote the marginal probability of the label
G^{(l)}=g
, and
p(G^{(l)}=g|\widetilde{X}^{(l)}=x)
